Trace Anomalies and the Graviton-Dilaton Amplitude
Denis Karateev, Zohar Komargodski, João Penedones, and Biswajit Sahoo

Abstract
We consider 3+1 dimensional Quantum Field Theories (QFTs) coupled to the dilaton and the graviton. We show that the graviton-dilaton scattering amplitude receives a universal contribution which is helicity flipping and is proportional to Δ ​ c − Δ ​ a \Delta c-\Delta a along any RG flow, where Δ ​ c \Delta c and Δ ​ a \Delta a are the differences of the UV and IR c c - and a a -trace anomalies respectively. This allows us to relate Δ ​ c − Δ ​ a \Delta c-\Delta a to spinning massive states in the spectrum of the QFT. We test our predictions in two simple examples: in the theory of a massive free scalar and in the theory of a massive Dirac fermion (a more complicated example is provided in a companion paper Karateev ). We discuss possible applications.



1
Introduction

Non-perturbative techniques in Quantum Field Theory (QFT) are of great interest, with applications ranging across many different branches of physics. One striking early example is due to ’t Hooft
hooft1980naturalness
who identified correlation functions which do not evolve under the renormalization group (RG)
Frishman:1980dq
.
These special protected correlation functions are related to anomalies of continuous global symmetries. In other words, ’t Hooft anomalies of global symmetries are RG independent quantities.
The scale independence of anomalies places interesting constraints on RG flows, commonly referred to as the “anomaly matching conditions.”

In this paper we will view QFT as an RG flow between a UV and an IR fixed points both of which are described by conformal field theories (CFTs). (The IR fixed point can be trivial if the theory is gapped.) The subject of this paper is to consider trace anomalies and explain how they should be matched. Trace anomalies are related to conformal symmetry, which is broken explicitly in theories with a mass scale. Usually, one does not discuss anomaly matching for symmetries which are explicitly broken.
Yet, for the case of conformal symmetry, there are protected amplitudes which we identify. The protected amplitudes that are determined by the
a
-trace anomaly were discussed in
Komargodski:2011vj
;
Komargodski:2011xv
, see also
Luty:2012ww
;
Baume:2014rla
. In particular, it was emphasized that if the QFT is coupled to a massless dilaton, the dilaton-dilaton scattering amplitude is universally proportional to
\Delta a\equiv a_{UV}-a_{IR}
at low energies.

In this work we extend this analysis by coupling the theory conformally to a massless graviton.
1
1

1

In
Gillioz:2019iye
the authors proposed interesting CFT sum-rules using the technology of background gravitational fields.
We identify protected correlators which are sensitive to the
c
-trace anomaly. Our analysis is motivated by a discussion in the literature on whether the
c
trace anomaly should be matched, see e.g.
Schwimmer:2010za
;
Nakayama:2017oye
;
Niarchos:2020nxk
;
Andriolo:2022lcb
;
Schwimmer:2023nzk
. We will give a simple argument proving that the answer is positive and demonstrate it explicitly in several examples.

We couple the graviton to the QFT with strength
\kappa
, which is the square root of Newton constant, and the dilaton couples as usual (canonically) to the trace of the energy momentum tensor with strength
1/f
.
The dilaton-dilaton scattering amplitude at low energies is given by

{\cal T}(s,t,u)=\frac{1}{f^{4}}\Delta a(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})~.

(1)

It is completely fixed by the change in the
a
anomaly and it is insensitive to the precise RG flow taking place, instead, it only depends on the end-points of the RG flow.

Our new result concerns with the graviton-dilaton amplitude at low energies, in which the graviton flips its helicty:

{\cal T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)={\kappa^{2}\over f^{2}}(\Delta c-\Delta a)t^{2},

(2)

where
\Delta c\equiv c_{UV}-c_{IR}
.
2
2

2

Sum-rules for
\Delta c
involving the stress-tensor two-point function and their implications in
d=2
space-times dimensions were discussed in
Cardy:1988tj
;
Hartman:2023ccw
and in
d\geq 2
space-time dimensions were discussed in
Cardy:1988cwa
;
Cappelli:1990yc
;
Cappelli:2001pz
;
Karateev:2020axc
. From these sum-rules one immediately obtains the
c
-theorem in
d=2

Zamolodchikov:1986gt
which states that
\Delta c\geq 0
. In
d>2
instead these sum-rules do not impose any constraint on the sign of
\Delta c
.

This result should hold in any QFT, regardless of whether it is gapped or not in the infrared. This amplitude is independent of the actual RG flow, as long as the end-points are fixed, as in (
1
).

Applying dispersion relations, we can use (
1
) to rewrite
\Delta a
in terms of the massive particles in the theory which couple to the trace of the energy-momentum tensor. All the massive particles appear in the corresponding sum rule.
Similarly, by using (
2
),
\Delta c-\Delta a
can be associated with spinning massive states (with a partial wave spin of at least 2) that couple to the trace of the energy-momentum tensor, under the assumption that the second derivative in
t
of the amplitude decays in the Regge limit:

\Delta c-\Delta a={f^{2}\over\kappa^{2}}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{\pi}\frac{\text{Im}\,\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{s}.

(3)

The combination
\Delta c-\Delta a
is not sign definite.

The combination
c-a
also appears in
DiPietro:2014bca
;
Beem:2017ooy
;
ArabiArdehali:2023bpq
in the context of counting specific operators in supersymmetric theories, as well as in the angular-dependent part of the expectation value of the energy in the state produced by the
U(1)_{R}
-curent
Hofman:2008ar
.
3
3

3

The
c-a
combination also appears in the computation of the logarithmic term in the entropy of Schwarszchild black
hole
Solodukhin:2010pk
.
Perhaps more relevant to our discussion here,
c-a
appeared in
Camanho:2014apa
, where it was related to spinning primary operators in the large central charge, strong coupling limit of CFTs. Here we are discussing
\Delta c-\Delta a
in the context of RG flows, and we have related it to spinning massive particles (and our discussion does not require large central charge or strong coupling), so the context is quite different, but there could be a relation.

A useful reformulation of (
1
) was recently unveiled in
Hartman:2023qdn
. There, one considers the state created by acting with the (appropriately smeared) trace of the energy-momentum on the vacuum,
T{}^{\mu}_{\mu}|{\rm VAC}\rangle
and one studies the null energy radiated off this state. It should be possible to think about (
2
) as
a helicity flipping process of a graviton propagating through this state. Another interesting quantity where the trace anomalies appear is the entanglement entropy in the vacuum
Solodukhin:2008dh
;
Casini:2017vbe
. It would be interesting to see if the combination
\Delta c-\Delta a
plays a similar central role in that setup.

The outline of this paper is as follows. In section
2
we review trace anomalies and outline the derivation of (
2
).
In section
3
we compute all the vertices of dilatons and gravitons that will eventually need to be assembled into scattering amplitudes.
In section
4
we compute the vertices explicitly for a massive fermion and massive boson, which we do to check the general predictions of section
3
. In section
5
we assemble all the vertices into scattering amplitudes of physical gravitons and dilatons, deriving (
2
). In section
6
we discuss less familiar trace anomalies, sometimes called “resonance” or “coupling space” anomalies – we will derive the low energy vertices protected by these anomalies and test our predictions in some simple RG flows.
In section
7
we list a few additional open questions. Three appendices cover technical material.

2
Review of Trace Anomalies and Outline of the Derivation of (
2
)

We will now review trace anomalies and explain the strategy behind deriving (
2
).

The discussion of trace anomalies and their matching can be done in the following context

•

Systems with exact conformal symmetry, which is spontaneously broken in the vacuum. This occurs in theories with a moduli space of degenerate vacua (mostly supersymmetric theories or large
N
theories). The infrared physics in the vacua with spontaneously broken conformal invariance can be nontrivial and the matching of trace anomalies is a powerful constraint on this physics.

The above setup is conceptually similar to anomaly matching of ordinary spontaneously broken global symmetries.
A more generic setup in which we can discuss the matching of trace anomalies is

•

Ordinary systems undergoing a renormalization group flow. They do not have conformal symmetry but the ideas of trace anomaly matching are still useful. Heuristically, one can see this in the following way. In the very weakly coupled regime of Nambu-Goldstone (i.e. in the limit of very large decay constant) it is impossible to tell apart the case of spontaneously broken symmetry from an explicitly broken one. Therefore, in the case of ordinary RG flows one still expects to be able to define interesting observables that are constrained by the ideas of anomaly matching and this leads to non-trivial results about generic renormalization group flows.

The two setups are quite similar and lead to similar conclusions. Our discussion will be framed in the realm of ordinary relativistic theories undergoing a renormalization group flow, i.e. the second item above, since it is more general.
4
4

4

One could also consider matching the trace anomaly in nontrivial states, and not just the relativistic invariant vacuum. We do not discuss it here but see
Eling:2013bj
;
Benjamin:2023qsc
for a discussion of how the trace anomaly is matched in the (disordered) thermal state.

As we will review, trace anomalies can be of “type A” or “type B”
Deser:1993yx
and we will see that they both need to be matched.

As we have mentioned, theories undergoing a renormalization group flow obviously explicitly break the conformal symmetry since there is an explicit mass scale
M
or more generally a set of mass scales
M_{i}
. The conformal symmetry of the ultraviolet fixed point nevertheless imposes constraints on various amplitudes of the theory. To tease out these facts we use a classical background field
\Omega(x)
(the dilaton) which is introduced in the original QFT by replacing all the mass scales
M_{i}
as

M_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}(x)\equiv M_{i}\,\Omega(x).

(4)

This is sometimes referred to as the Stueckelberg trick.
This includes all mass scales, whether they are generated classically or quantum mechanically.
We also place the QFT on a curved non-dynamical background with metric
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
.
The partition function of the theory
Z[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
now enjoys Weyl invariance if a Weyl transformation of the metric is accompanied by an appropriate transformation of
\Omega
, namely

\Omega(x)\to e^{-\sigma(x)}\Omega(x),\qquad g_{\mu\nu}(x)\to e^{2\sigma(x)}g_{\mu\nu}(x),

(5)

where
\sigma(x)
is the coordinate dependent parameter of the Weyl transformation.
We can say that
\Omega(x)
is a “spurion” for the conformal symmetry. Also, one can think of
\log\Omega(x)
as the Nambu-Goldstone boson of spontaneous conformal symmetry breaking. Clearly, the background field
\Omega(x)
couples to the trace of the energy-momentum tensor (accompanied by additional nonlinear couplings that are necessary to ensure conformal invariance) and hence amplitudes involving
\Omega(x)
are just a bookkeeping device for correlators of the trace of the energy-momentum tensor.

In this paper we focus on
d=4
space-time dimensions and work in mostly plus metric
\eta^{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}\{-1,+1,+1,+1\}
. Instead of the partition function it is more convenient to work with the connected functional
W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
related to the partition function as

Z[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]=e^{iW[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]}.

(6)

When we say that the theory is Weyl invariant this is up to the following variation of the connected functional

\delta_{\sigma}W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\sigma(x)\left(-a_{\text{UV}}E_{4}+c_{\text{UV}}\mathcal{W}^{2}\right),

(7)

where
E_{4}
is the Euler density and
\mathcal{W}^{2}
is the square of the Weyl tensor. For our conventions see appendix
A
.
The coefficients
a_{\text{UV}}
and
c_{\text{UV}}
are called the
a
- and
c
-trace anomalies (for a review of the history of trace anomalies, see
Duff:1993wm
). They also appear as the OPE coefficients in the UV CFT three-point function of the stress-tensor
Osborn:1993cr
;
Costa:2011mg
.
5
5

5

An alternative to (
7
) is to express the right hand side as the trace of the stress tensor of the UV CFT:

\delta_{\sigma}W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ \sigma(x)\,\langle 0|T{}^{\mu}_{\mu}(x)|0\rangle_{g}^{\text{UV CFT}}.

(8)

In (
7
), if we choose the infinitesimal Weyl transformation parameter
\sigma(x)
to be independent of coordinates (i.e. a constant scale transformation), the anomaly contribution associated with the
E_{4}
reduces to a boundary term. For this reason, the
a
-trace anomaly is a “type-A” anomaly. Conversely, the
\mathcal{W}^{2}
term remains present for a constant
\sigma(x)
, which makes it a “type-B” anomaly.
It is important to keep in mind that there could be additional
c
-number contributions on the right hand side of (
7
), for instance, if there are additional background fields (coupling constants). We will discuss some of these generalizations later. Such additional terms have to be analytic in the background fields, similarly to (
7
).

The most important property of the Weyl anomaly (
7
) is that it remains invariant along the whole RG flow (for any energy scale and for arbitrary background fields). In order for this to be true it is crucial that the Weyl invariance is only violated by a
c
-number, i.e. a functional of the background fields. This is why
a_{\text{UV}}
and
c_{\text{UV}}
are pure numbers determined by the conformal anomalies at short distances, and independent of the precise trajectory of the RG flow, or the state of the system in the infrared. This universality is the reason that equation (
7
) is powerful.
The connected functional (
6
) can be equivalently written in terms of the low energy degrees of freedom. It will then depend on the low energy effective action
A_{\text{EFT}}
.
Equation (
7
) places constraints on the possible dependence of the IR effective action
A_{\text{EFT}}
on the background fields
\Omega(x)
and
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
. The Weyl anomaly of the connected functional written in terms of the
A_{\text{EFT}}
must be the same as (
7
). This is the Weyl anomaly matching condition which we will use shortly.

A technically crucial point is that the connected functional
W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
is not entirely well defined and neither is its variation
\delta_{\sigma}W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
. As usual, the locality of the underlying theory allows us to parameterize the ambiguity in
W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
by local terms,

A_{\text{local}}\equiv\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{local}}(M_{i}(x),g_{\mu\nu}(x)),

(9)

where
M_{i}(x)
was defined in (
4
).
A_{\text{local}}
cannot contain nonlocal terms such as
\log M_{i}(x)
since
\mathcal{L}_{local}
represents contact terms between local operators to which
M_{i}(x)
and
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
couple. Notice, that
A_{\text{local}}
is not required to be Weyl invariant and hence may contribute to the variation

\delta_{\sigma}W[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu}]
. Therefore, when we write (
7
) this must be considered only up to terms of the form
\delta_{\sigma}A_{\text{local}}
. Alternatively, one can imagine always working in a scheme where (
7
) holds true.

Before addressing general RG flows let us first recall how the variation (
7
) is satisfied in a conformal field theory. In this case the partition function does not depend on
\Omega(x)
since there are no mass scales to start with. The partition function of a CFT as a functional of
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
is far too complicated to be determined, but
Deser:1993yx
and
Deser:1976yx
have constructed particular non-local terms which lead to the right variation for the
a
- and
c
-anomalies:
6
6

6

In the literature, there are many different constructions of the
a
-anomaly functional, whose variations produce (
7
). These constructions are related by some Weyl invariant terms and local terms in the functional, as recently discussed in
Barvinsky:2023exr
;
Barvinsky:2023aae
.

W_{\text{UV CFT}}^{\text{a-part}}[g_{\mu\nu}]=\frac{1}{9}\int d^{4}x\ \sqrt{-g}\ \square^{-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R+10R_{\mu\nu}R^{\nu\alpha}R_{\alpha}^{\ \mu}\\
-13R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}R+\frac{41}{18}R^{3}+6R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}R^{\mu\alpha}R^{\nu\beta}\Big].

(10)

W_{\text{UV CFT}}^{\text{c-part}}[g_{\mu\nu}]=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\ \sqrt{-g}\ \mathcal{W}(x)\ln\left(\frac{\square}{\mu^{2}}\right)\mathcal{W}(x)+\cdots.

(11)

The ellipses in (
11
) represent all higher-order curvature corrections to the expression. These corrections are necessary to ensure that the Laplacian in the logarithm’s argument becomes homogeneous under a Weyl transformation when it acts on the Weyl tensor. The nonlocal expressions in (
10
) and (
11
) should be used only for background fields with compact support where the differential operators are invertible. They reproduce the required variation (
7
) when the coefficient of (
10
) is
-a_{\text{UV}}
and the coefficient of (
11
) is
c_{\text{UV}}
. The full effective action could contain additional, generally nonlocal, Weyl invariant terms (as well as scheme dependent local counter-terms which may or may not be Weyl invariant as discussed below (
9
)). Using (
10
) and (
11
) we can compute correlation functions of the stress-tensor.
The merit of such nonlocal effective actions is that they allow to derive interesting identities satisfied by the energy-momentum tensor correlation functions.
For instance, one can see from (
11
) that the two-point function of stress-tensors in flat spacetime is proportional to
c_{\text{UV}}
. Instead,
a_{\text{UV}}
influences only the three-(and higher-) point functions of stress-tensors in flat spacetime, simply because the expansion of (
10
) around flat space starts from three gravitons.

Now let us present the case of a generic renormalization group flow (or, similarly, conformal field theories in a vacuum with spontaneously broken conformal invariance). We will present a local IR effective action that depends on
\Omega(x)\equiv e^{-\tau(x)}
and
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
whose variation leads to (
7
). Let us consider three distinct terms of the IR effective action which obey the following constraints

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}A_{a}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\sigma(x)E_{4},

(12)

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}A_{c}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\sigma(x)\mathcal{W}^{2},

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}A_{\text{invariant}}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=0.

The solution to these constraints was found in
Fradkin:1983tg
;
Schwimmer:2010za
using the Wess–Zumino construction
Wess:1971yu
, see also
Komargodski:2011vj
. It reads
7
7

7

Notice that the IR effective action is local in
\tau(x)
and
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
, since in the infrared we expand around a non-zero value of
\Omega(x)
which sets the scale of the RG flow. This situation should be contrasted with the scheme dependent terms (
9
) which must be local in the UV couplings instead.

\displaystyle A_{a}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\Big(\tau E_{4}+4\left(R^{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}R\right)\partial_{\mu}\tau\partial_{\nu}\tau+2(\partial\tau)^{4}-4(\partial\tau)^{2}\square\tau\Big),

(13)

\displaystyle A_{c}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,\tau\,\mathcal{W}^{2},

(14)

\displaystyle A_{\text{invariant}}[\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\ \sqrt{-\widehat{g}}\Bigg(M^{4}\lambda+M^{2}r_{0}\widehat{R}\ +\ r_{1}\widehat{R}^{2}+r_{2}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{2}+r_{3}\widehat{E}_{4}+\ldots\Bigg).

(15)

Here
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)=e^{-2\tau(x)}g_{\mu\nu}(x)
, which is a Weyl invariant combination.
We have introduced the dimensionless parameters
\lambda
,
r_{0}
,
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
and
r_{3}
whose values depend on a particular theory (and they could depend on the precise RG flow, even if the ultraviolet and infrared fixed points are the same). The parameter
M
is the mass gap.
Here,
\widehat{R}_{\mu\nu}
,
\widehat{R}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{2}
and
\widehat{E}_{4}
are the Ricci tensor, Ricci scalar, squared Weyl tensor and the Euler density which are constructed from the metric
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
.

Let us first suppose for simplicity that our theory is gapped in the IR. Then due to (
12
) the most general IR effective action which lead to the same Weyl anomaly as (
7
) is given by

A_{\text{EFT}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]=-a_{\text{UV}}\times A_{a}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]+c_{\text{UV}}\times A_{c}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]\ +\ A_{\text{invariant}}[\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}].

(16)

In other words we can say that we have constructed the IR effective action (
16
) by matching the UV Weyl anomaly (
7
). If instead our theory in the IR is described by a non-trivial CFT with trace anomalies
a_{\text{IR}}
and
c_{\text{IR}}
then the form of the effective action is

\displaystyle A_{\text{EFT}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu},\Theta]=

\displaystyle-\Delta a\times A_{a}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]+\Delta c\times A_{c}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]+A_{\text{invariant}}[\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle+A_{\text{IR CFT}}[\Omega,g_{\mu\nu},\Theta]~.

(17)

The first line has the same structure as (
16
).
The second line describes the IR CFT with (schematically)
dynamical
fields denoted by
\Theta
interacting with the metric and dilaton.
The differences of the UV and IR anomalies are defined as
8
8

8

In the case of QFTs with of spontaneous conformal symmetry breaking the dilaton is a physical particle. More precisely it is the Goldstone boson of the spontaneous symmetry breaking. Then in the IR the trace anomalies are given by
a_{\text{IR}}=a_{\text{IR CFT}}+a_{\text{free boson}}
and
c_{\text{IR}}=c_{\text{IR CFT}}+c_{\text{free boson}}
.

\Delta a\equiv a_{\text{UV}}-a_{\text{IR}},\qquad\Delta c\equiv c_{\text{UV}}-c_{\text{IR}}.

(18)

The IR effective action (
17
) is constructed to precisely reproduce the Weyl anomaly (
7
). In the special case when the IR theory is gapped, namely when
a_{\text{IR}}=0
and
c_{\text{IR}}=0
, the effective action (
17
) simply reduces to (
16
).

The effective action (
17
) can be used in practice in the following way. One can take variations of this action with respect to background fields in order to compute vertices of the background fields. Some special vertices are universal quantities which are sensitive to
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
only. Particular care should be given to the
A_{\text{IR CFT}}
term in (
16
) as was emphasized also in
Nakayama:2011wq
;
Luty:2012ww
. It turns out that for some special choices of the background fields and vertices, one can safely ignore
A_{\text{IR CFT}}
. We will see that the garviton-graviton-dilaton three-point vertex and the graviton-graviton-dilaton-dilaton four-point vertex are sufficient to universally extract
\Delta c,\Delta a
from the multiple tensor structures in these vertices. We will then combine the vertices into scattering amplitudes and derive (
2
).

3
Vertices of Gravitons and Dilatons

Recall from the previous section, that the effective action describing the probe fields is given by the equation (
17
) together with (
13
) - (
15
).
Let us now rewrite it using fields
\varphi(x)
and
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
defined as

\displaystyle e^{-\tau(x)}

\displaystyle\equiv 1-\frac{\varphi(x)}{\sqrt{2}f},

(19)

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv\eta_{\mu\nu}+2\kappa\,h_{\mu\nu}(x).

We will call
\varphi(x)
and
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
the dilaton and the graviton fields respectively.
Here we have introduced dimensionful parameters
f
and
\kappa
with mass dimensions
[f]=+1
and
[\kappa]=-1
. We use
f^{-1}
and
\kappa
as expansion parameters, which allow to keep track of the fields
\varphi(x)
and
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
. The fields
\varphi(x)
and
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
at this stage are used simply as bookkeeping devices for correlators of the energy momentum tensor.
Below, we will impose various constraints on
\varphi(x)
and
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
which are compatible with the on-shell conditions we will eventually impose when the fields will become propagating and not just background fields.

In subsection
3.1
we define vertices of probe fields (dilatons and gravitons). In subsection
3.2
we will compute these vertices from the effective action (
17
). The latter will depend on
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
as well as various parameters that are not related to the anomalies – we show that certain kinematical structures are universal and depend on the anomalies only. We show that this occurs for vertices of three dilatons, four dilatons, two gravitons and one dilaton, one graviton and two dilatons, and finally, two gravitons and two dilatons.

3.1
Definition of Vertices

We define the Fourier transforms

\varphi(x)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\exp(ix\cdot k)\varphi(k),\qquad h_{\mu\nu}(x)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\exp(ix\cdot k)h_{\mu\nu}(k).

(20)

The vertex in Fourier space which contains
m
gravitons and
n
dilatons is defined as the following variation

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{m}+q_{1}+\ldots+q_{n})\times V^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}\nu_{m}}_{(h\ldots h\varphi\ldots\varphi)}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m},q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})\equiv\\
(2\pi)^{4(m+n)}\frac{i\,\delta^{m+n}A_{\text{EFT}}}{\delta h_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(p_{1})\ldots\delta h_{\mu_{m}\nu_{m}}(p_{m})\delta\varphi(q_{1})\ldots\delta\varphi(q_{n})}\Bigg|_{h,\varphi=0}.

(21)

In order to compute variations we use the following formulas

\frac{\delta\varphi(p)}{\delta\varphi(k)}=\delta^{(4)}(p-k),\qquad\frac{\delta h_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}(p)}{\delta h_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}(k)}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta^{\nu_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\delta^{\nu_{2}}_{\mu_{2}}+\delta^{\nu_{2}}_{\mu_{1}}\delta^{\nu_{1}}_{\mu_{2}})\times\delta^{(4)}(p-k).

(22)

It is very convenient to define the index-free vertices as follows

V_{(h\ldots h\varphi\ldots\varphi)}\equiv\\
\varepsilon_{1,\,\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\ldots\varepsilon_{m,\,\mu_{m}\nu_{m}}\times V^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}\nu_{m}}_{(h\ldots h\varphi\ldots\varphi)}.

(23)

Here
\varepsilon_{i}
are auxiliary complex polarization parameters.
Throughout this paper we will work in the harmonic gauge

\partial^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}h=0.

(24)

This allows to significantly simplify all the expressions. Furthermore, we will assume that polarizations are traceless and transverse, namely

\eta^{\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{i,\,\mu\nu}=0,\qquad k_{i}^{\mu}\varepsilon_{i,\,\mu\nu}=0.

(25)

These restrictions can be chosen without loss of generality for the following reason: diffeomorphism invariance of the effective action
A_{\text{EFT}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]
implies that under infinitesimal coordinate transformations
\delta x^{\mu}=\chi^{\mu}(x)
, the dilaton and the metric transform as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\delta\varphi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\chi^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\varphi

(26)

\displaystyle\delta h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\left(\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}\right)-\left(h_{\rho\nu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{\rho}+h_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{\rho}+\chi^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\right),

(27)

and together with (
5
), this allows us to remove the restrictions above.

For our purposes we will need to consider the following five vertices

V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)},\qquad V^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{(h\varphi\varphi)},\qquad V_{(hh\varphi)},\qquad V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi)},\qquad V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}.

(28)

Notice that all the vertices above, except for the second one, are contracted with polarizations. This stresses the fact that for the computation of
V^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
we do not use (
25
).
Below we provide the diagrammatic notation for all the vertices (
28
).

3.2
Computations of Vertices

In this section we will compute the vertices (
28
) using the effective action (
17
). The main results of this section are given by equations (
30
), (
32
), (
35
), (
40
) and (
44
).

In what follows we will write some vertices with additional constraints such as
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
and
\partial^{2}h_{\mu\nu}(x)=0
. When this is done we carefully emphasise this in the text. At this point these constraints are not the equations of motion but simply a particular choice for the background field configuration which simplifies the expressions.

3.2.1
Three-Point Vertices

Let us start by writing the part of the effective action (
17
) which contains three dilaton fields
\varphi
and four derivatives

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\text{EFT}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta a\frac{\sqrt{2}}{f^{3}}\int d^{4}x\ (\partial\varphi)^{2}\partial^{2}\varphi+r_{1}\frac{18\sqrt{2}}{f^{3}}\int d^{4}x\ \varphi(\partial^{2}\varphi)^{2}+\ldots.

(29)

We obtain

V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{f^{3}}\bigg(\Delta a\left(\left(k_{1}^{2}\right)^{2}+\left(k_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}+\left(k_{3}^{2}\right)^{2}\right)\\
+2(18r_{1}-\Delta a)\left(k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}k_{1}^{2}\right)+\ldots\bigg),

(30)

where
k_{3}=-k_{1}-k_{2}
. The ellipsis denote terms of different powers of momenta coming from other derivative terms in the effective action. If we impose
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
the
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
vertex vanishes.

Now consider the four derivative part of the EFT action which contains two dilatons and one graviton. It reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\text{EFT}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\kappa\Delta a}{f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\left(\partial^{2}h^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}h\eta^{\mu\nu}\right)\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi

(31)

\displaystyle+\frac{r_{1}\kappa}{f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\left(18h\partial^{2}\varphi\partial^{2}\varphi-72h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\varphi\partial^{2}\varphi-6\partial^{2}h\varphi\partial^{2}\varphi\right).

From the above action the 2-dilaton-1-graviton EFT vertex becomes

\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa}{f^{2}}\left(6M^{4}\lambda\eta^{\mu\nu}+M^{2}r_{0}\left(\eta^{\mu\nu}(k_{1}^{2}+3k_{2}^{2}+3k_{3}^{2})-6(k_{2}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}+k_{3}^{\mu}k_{3}^{\nu})\right)\right)

(32)

\displaystyle+\frac{2i\kappa\Delta a}{f^{2}}k_{1}^{2}\,\left(k_{2}^{\mu}k^{\nu}_{3}+k^{\nu}_{2}k^{\mu}_{3}-\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{2}.k_{3}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{ir_{1}\kappa}{f^{2}}\left(36\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}-6\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{1}^{2}(k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2})-72(k^{\mu}_{2}k^{\nu}_{2}k_{3}^{2}+k^{\mu}_{3}k^{\nu}_{3}k_{2}^{2})\right).

Here for completeness we have added terms with zero and two powers of momenta coming from the terms in the Lagrangian with zero and two derivatives. Due to momentum conservation we have
k_{3}=-k_{1}-k_{2}
as before. Here we do not impose on-shell conditions on the dilaton or the metric since this particular vertex will be useful when these are off-shell. By using momentum conservation and the harmonic gauge the four-momentum part of the equation (
32
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\kappa}{f^{2}}\eta^{\mu\nu}\Big[-12r_{1}k_{1}^{2}k_{1}.k_{2}+(2\Delta a-12r_{1})k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}+36r_{1}(k_{2}^{2})^{2}-6r_{1}(k_{1}^{2})^{2}\Big]

(33)

\displaystyle-\frac{i\kappa}{f^{2}}k_{2}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}\Big[(4\Delta a+72r_{1})k_{1}^{2}+144r_{1}(k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}.k_{2})\Big].

In order to implement harmonic gauge (
24
), one replaces terms of the form

k_{1}^{\mu}A^{\nu}
or
k_{1}^{\nu}A^{\mu}
by
\frac{1}{2}(k_{1}.A)\eta^{\mu\nu}
in (
32
) to bring it to the above form. The above expression of graviton-dilaton-dilaton vertex is closely related to the ANEC construction of
Hartman:2023qdn
. It turns out that the projection operator
(\partial_{k_{2u}}-\partial_{k_{3u}})^{2}\vec{\partial}_{k_{2}}\cdot\vec{\partial}_{k_{3}}
of
Hartman:2023qdn
, when applied to the vertex (
33
) after setting
k_{1}=-k_{2}-k_{3}
, yields only
\Delta a
, hence it is consistent with the sum rule.

Let us now consider the part of the effective action (
17
) which contains two gravitons and one dilaton, and four derivatives. It reads as

A_{\text{EFT}}=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\int d^{4}x\ \Big(2(2\Delta a-\Delta c)\varphi\partial^{2}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}h^{\mu\nu}\\
+(-\Delta a+\Delta c)\varphi\left(4\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\nu}h^{\mu\sigma}+4\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}h^{\nu\rho}-8\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\rho}h^{\nu\sigma}\right)\\
+12r_{1}\partial^{2}\varphi\big(4h^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}h_{\alpha\beta}+3\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\mu}h^{\alpha\beta}-2\partial^{\beta}h^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}\big)\Big).

(34)

We obtain

V_{(hh\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=\\
f_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})+f_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})+f_{3}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2}),

(35)

where the functions
f_{i}(k_{1},k_{2})
read as

\displaystyle f_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{4i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\Big(2(-\Delta a+\Delta c+18r_{1})(k_{1}.k_{2})^{2}+(2\Delta a-\Delta c+24r_{1})k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}

(36)

\displaystyle+12r_{1}(k_{1}^{4}+k_{2}^{4})+42r_{1}(k_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})+\ldots\Big),

\displaystyle f_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{8i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\left(-\Delta a+\Delta c+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle f_{3}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{8i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\left(2(\Delta a-\Delta c-6r_{1})(k_{1}.k_{2})-6r_{1}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})+\ldots\right).

We used
k_{3}=-k_{1}-k_{2}
. Again the ellipsis denote the terms which are not contributing at four powers of momenta.
In the above we have used the following short-hand notation

(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})\equiv\varepsilon_{1\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\mu\nu},\quad(p_{i}.\varepsilon_{j}.p_{k})\equiv p_{i\mu}\varepsilon_{j}^{\mu\nu}p_{k\nu},\quad(p_{i}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.p_{j})\equiv p_{i\mu}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\rho}\varepsilon_{2\rho\nu}p_{j}^{\nu}.

(37)

The vertex (
35
) is invariant under the
1\leftrightarrow 2
exchange as expected (this is due to
(p_{i}.\varepsilon_{j}.p_{k})=(p_{k}.\varepsilon_{j}.p_{i})
,
(p_{i}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.p_{j})=(p_{j}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.p_{i})
).
Unlike the
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
vertex, we see that (
34
) does not trivialize upon imposing
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
. Only the term proportional to
r_{1}
trivializes, so after imposing
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
we find

\displaystyle f_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{4i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\Big(2(-\Delta a+\Delta c)(k_{1}.k_{2})^{2}+(2\Delta a-\Delta c)k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}+\ldots\Big),

(38)

\displaystyle f_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{8i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\left(-\Delta a+\Delta c+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle f_{3}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{16i\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\,\left((\Delta a-\Delta c)(k_{1}.k_{2})+\ldots\right).

Additionally, imposing
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
removes contributions from the infrared CFT as we show in appendix
B
. We remained with three distinct kinematical structures that depend on different combinations of the
a
- and
c
-anomalies only.

3.2.2
Four-point Vertices

The part of the effective action (
17
) which contains four dilatons and only four derivatives and with the constraint
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
has the following simple form

A_{\text{EFT}}=\Delta a\frac{1}{2f^{4}}\int d^{4}x\ (\partial\varphi)^{4}+\ldots.

(39)

We obtain the following four-point vertex

V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4})=\frac{i\Delta a}{f^{4}}\left(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2}\right)+\ldots.

(40)

Here we have used the Mandelstam variables defined as

s\equiv-(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2},\qquad t\equiv-(k_{1}-k_{3})^{2},\qquad u\equiv-(k_{1}-k_{4})^{2}.

(41)

Notice that we have written the vertex (
40
) in such a way that the momenta
k_{3}
and
k_{4}
can be interpreted as the momenta of outgoing particles. We also have the standard relations that stem from momentum conservation and our choice of background fields
k_{i}^{2}=0,s+t+u=0
. Since
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
vanishes when
\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0
is imposed, it is immediate to see that the physical dilaton-dilaton scattering amplitude is then given by (
1
).

The part of the effective action (
17
) which contains two gravitons and two dilatons and four derivatives has the form

A_{\text{EFT}}=(-\Delta a+\Delta c)\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\ \varphi(x)^{2}\Big(4\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\nu}h^{\mu\sigma}+4\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}h^{\nu\rho}-8\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\rho}h^{\nu\sigma}\Big)\\
-\Delta a\frac{2\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\ \partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi\Big(\partial^{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\nu}h^{\alpha\beta}+2h^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}h_{\alpha\beta}-2h^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\beta}h_{\alpha}^{\mu}-2h^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}h_{\alpha}^{\nu}\\
+2h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}h^{\mu\nu}+2\partial^{\beta}h^{\nu}_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}h^{\mu\alpha}-2\partial_{\beta}h^{\nu}_{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}h^{\mu\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\big(3\partial_{\rho}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\rho}h^{\alpha\beta}-2\partial^{\beta}h^{\rho\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\rho\beta}\big)\Big)\\
+r_{1}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\ \Big(144\ h^{\mu\nu}h^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\varphi\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\varphi\Big)+\ldots

(42)

In writing (
42
) we have used

\partial^{2}h_{\mu\nu}(x)=0~,\quad\partial^{2}\varphi(x)=0~.

(43)

We obtain the following form of the vertex

V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=g_{1}(s,t,u)\times(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})+g_{2}(s,t,u)\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})\\
+g_{3}(s,t,u)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})+g_{3}(s,u,t)\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})+g_{4}(s,t,u)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\\
+g_{5}(s,t,u)\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})+g_{6}(s,t,u)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})\\
+g_{6}(s,u,t)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})+g_{7}(s,t,u)\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})\\
+g_{8}(s,t,u)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})+g_{9}(s,t,u)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\\
+g_{9}(s,u,t)\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3}),

(44)

where the functions
g_{i}(s,t,u)
read as

\displaystyle g_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-36M^{4}\lambda-9M^{2}r_{0}s+3(-\Delta a+\Delta c)s^{2}+3\Delta a(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})+\ldots\right),

(45)

\displaystyle g_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(12M^{2}r_{0}+12(-\Delta a+\Delta c)s+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle g_{3}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-72M^{2}r_{0}-24\Delta a\,u+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle g_{4}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(144M^{2}r_{0}-24\Delta a\,s+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle g_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(12(-\Delta a+\Delta c)+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle g_{6}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(24\Delta a+432r_{1}+\ldots\right),\qquad g_{7}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-48\Delta a+\ldots\right),

\displaystyle g_{8}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(864r_{1}+\ldots\right),\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;g_{9}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-864r_{1}+\ldots\right).

Here for completeness we added terms with zero and two powers of momenta.
The ellipsis denote the terms with higher powers of momenta. Using the definitions in (
37
), it is straightforward to check that the result (
44
) is symmetric under the
1\leftrightarrow 2
exchange.

We see that
\Delta a,\Delta c
appear in multiple kinematical structures of the four-point function. An important property shown in appendix
B
is that the contributions from the infrared CFT all have the same kinematical dependence as the term proportional to
r_{1}
in (
42
). Therefore, our prediction for how
\Delta a,\Delta c
appear in the four-point vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
is robust.

4
Examples of
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
from Dilaton-Graviton Vertices

Let us consider two simple examples: the theory of a free massive scalar and the theory of a free massive Dirac fermion. In the UV the mass terms are relevant, thus in the UV these theories are described by free conformal field theories. The respective values of the
a
- and
c
-anomalies have been reported in
Osborn:1993cr
. In the IR both theories are empty, which means that both anomalies are zero. Using the results of
Osborn:1993cr
we can write then

\displaystyle\text{free scalar:}

\displaystyle\Delta a=\frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad\Delta c=\frac{3}{5760\pi^{2}},

(46)

\displaystyle\text{free Dirac fermion:}

\displaystyle\Delta a=\frac{11}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad\Delta c=\frac{18}{5760\pi^{2}}.

In this section we couple these theories to the dilaton field
\Omega(x)
and place these theories on a curved background with the metric
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
. We then compute some of the vertices (
28
). Comparing the result with the predictions provided in section
3.2
we can extract the values of
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
. We will see that the obtained values are in a perfect agreement with the known values (
46
) and the structure of the vertices is in accord with our expectations.

We first report our results in
d
space-time dimensions. This will be important for regularizing divergent loop integrals in
d=4
. The final results for the vertices will be given in
d=4
.

In the computations below we will use the following relations

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}f(\ell^{2})\ell^{\mu_{1}}\ell^{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{d}\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}f(\ell^{2})\ell^{2},

(47)

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}f(\ell^{2})\ell^{\mu_{1}}\ell^{\mu_{2}}\ell^{\mu_{3}}\ell^{\mu_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\eta^{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\eta^{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{4}}\eta^{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}}{d(d+2)}\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}f(\ell^{2})\left(\ell^{2}\right)^{2}.

Similar integrals which involve an odd number of
\ell^{\mu}
momenta vanish.
Finally, we will need the following scalar integrals

\mathbf{J}\left(a,b;\Delta\right)\equiv\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{(\ell^{2})^{a}}{\left(\ell^{2}+\Delta-i\epsilon\right)^{b}}=\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{1}{\Delta^{b-a-\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(a+\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(b-a-\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(b)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}.

(48)

4.1
Free Massive Scalar

Recall the standard action for the free massive scalar

A_{\text{free scalar}}[\Phi]\equiv\int d^{4}x\left(-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x)\partial_{\nu}\Phi(x)-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\Phi^{2}(x)\right).

(49)

Adding the dilaton field and placing this theory on a
d
-dimensional curved background we obtain the following action

A^{\text{compensated}}_{\text{free scalar}}[\Phi,\varphi,h]\equiv\int d^{d}x\ \sqrt{-g}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial_{\nu}\Phi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}e^{-2\tau}\Phi^{2}-\frac{d-2}{8(d-1)}R\Phi^{2}\Bigg).

(50)

Let us now evaluate the Feynman rules for the action (
50
).
We will denote by solid lines massive scalar particles, by dashed lines dilatons and by wavy lines gravitons.
We obtain

\varepsilon_{3}
\varepsilon_{4}
p_{1}
p_{2}
k_{1}
k_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3}\varepsilon_{3}^{\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{4}^{\alpha\beta}\textbf{Sym}_{(\mu\nu),(\alpha\beta)}\Big(6\eta_{\mu\alpha}\eta_{\nu\beta}(-p_{1}.p_{2}+m^{2})+6\eta_{\nu\alpha}(p_{1\mu}p_{2\beta}+p_{1\beta}p_{2\mu})

\displaystyle+6\eta_{\mu\beta}(p_{1\nu}p_{2\alpha}+p_{1\alpha}p_{2\nu})+\frac{3(d-2)}{2(d-1)}\left((2k_{1}^{2}+2k_{2}^{2}+3k_{1}.k_{2})\eta_{\mu\alpha}\eta_{\nu\beta}-2\eta_{\mu\alpha}k_{1\beta}k_{2\nu}\right)\Big).

In writing these vertices we did not make any further assumptions.

The simplest three-point vertex is the three dilaton vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
which has been evaluated in

Komargodski:2011vj
. We will not discuss it further here. Instead below we focus on the vertices
V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
and
V_{(hh\varphi)}
. We will conclude this section by computing the four-point vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
.

4.1.1
Graviton-Dilaton-Dilaton Vertex

The three-point vertex
V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
in the theory of massive scalar is given by the sum of the following four diagrams

\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\hbox to102.15pt{\vbox to70.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.51044pt\lower-30.37872pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{}}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 120.5 -41.76 L 78.74 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

We can now use the above Feynman diagrams to compute this vertex. Let us stress that we will not make assumptions of transversality and tracelessness in the computation below (but we will employ the harmonic gauge, when said so explicitly).

The first diagram in (
51
) reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{1\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa m^{2}}{2f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\Bigg[\eta_{\mu\nu}\left\{\left(1-\frac{2}{d}\right)\mathbf{J}(1,2;\Delta_{1})+k_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{d-2}{4(d-1)}-x(1-x)\right)\mathbf{J}(0,2;\Delta_{1})\right\}

(52)

\displaystyle+\eta_{\mu\nu}m^{2}\mathbf{J}(0,2;\Delta_{1})+2x(1-x)k_{1\mu}k_{1\nu}\mathbf{J}(0,2;\Delta_{1})\Bigg],

where
\Delta_{1}=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2}
.
The second diagram in (
51
) reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{2\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4\kappa m^{4}}{f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \Bigg[\eta_{\mu\nu}\left\{\left(1-\frac{2}{d}\right)\mathbf{J}(1,3;\Delta_{2})+m^{2}\mathbf{J}(0,3;\Delta_{2})\right\}

(53)

\displaystyle+\eta_{\mu\nu}\left\{-k_{1}^{2}x(1-x)+k_{3}^{3}y^{2}+y(1-2x)k_{1}.k_{3}+\frac{d-2}{4(d-1)}k_{1}^{2}\right\}\mathbf{J}(0,3;\Delta_{2})

\displaystyle+\left\{(k_{1}x-k_{3}y)_{\mu}\big((1-x)k_{1}+k_{3}y\big)_{\nu}+(k_{1}x-k_{3}y)_{\nu}\big((1-x)k_{1}+k_{3}y\big)_{\mu}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\ \mathbf{J}(0,3;\Delta_{2})\Bigg],

where
\Delta_{2}=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2}+y(1-y)k_{3}^{2}+2xy(k_{1}\cdot k_{3})
.
The third diagram in (
51
) reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{3\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa m^{4}}{f^{2}}\ \eta_{\mu\nu}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \mathbf{J}(0,2;\Delta_{3}),

(54)

where
\Delta_{3}=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{2}^{2}
.
The fourth diagram in (
51
) reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{4\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa m^{4}}{f^{2}}\ \eta_{\mu\nu}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \mathbf{J}(0,2;\Delta_{4}),

(55)

where
\Delta_{4}=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{3}^{2}
. The final expression for the one-graviton-two-dilaton vertex is given by the following sum

\displaystyle\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=V_{1}^{\mu\nu}+V_{2}^{\mu\nu}+V_{3}^{\mu\nu}+V_{4}^{\mu\nu}.

(56)

The low energy expansion of (
56
) after substituting
k_{3}=-k_{1}-k_{2}
and picking up the fourth power of the momenta is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}\eta^{\mu\nu}\Big[2k_{1}^{2}\left(4k_{1}.k_{2}+5k_{2}^{2}\right)+7(k_{1}^{2})^{2}-2\big(4(k_{1}.k_{2})^{2}+6k_{1}.k_{2}k_{2}^{2}

(57)

\displaystyle+3(k_{2}^{2})^{2}\big)\Big]+\frac{4i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}k_{1}^{\mu}k_{1}^{\nu}\left(3k_{1}^{2}+6k_{1}.k_{2}+5k_{2}^{2}\right)-\frac{8i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}(2k_{1}^{2}+3k_{1}.k_{2}

\displaystyle+3k_{2}^{2})k_{2}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}-\frac{2i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}(3k_{1}^{2}+4k_{1}.k_{2}+6k_{2}^{2})(k_{1}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}+k_{1}^{\nu}k_{2}^{\mu}).

In harmonic gauge
\partial^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}h=0
for the graviton the above expression can be brought to the following form

\displaystyle\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}\eta^{\mu\nu}\Big[2k_{1}^{2}k_{1}.k_{2}+(k_{1}^{2})^{2}-6(k_{2}^{2})^{2}\Big]

(58)

\displaystyle-\frac{8i\kappa}{5760\pi^{2}f^{2}}(2k_{1}^{2}+3k_{1}.k_{2}+3k_{2}^{2})k_{2}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}.

Now recall that the EFT prediction for the one-graviton-two-dilaton vertex was given in (
33
).
Comparing the above expression with our computation (
58
) we conclude that

\Delta a=\frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad r_{1}=\frac{\Delta a}{6}.

(59)

This is in a perfect agreement with the first line in (
46
).

4.1.2
Graviton-Graviton-Dilaton Vertex

In this example this vertex is given by the sum of the following two diagrams

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{1}-\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell
\ell

Let us denote these diagrams by
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
respectively. Then

V_{(hh\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=V_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})+V_{2}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}).

(60)

The first diagram reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2}\kappa^{2}m^{2}}{6f}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(61)

\displaystyle\Big[\eta_{\mu\alpha}\eta_{\nu\beta}\Big\{6(\ell^{2}+m^{2})-6\ell.(k_{1}+k_{2})+\frac{3(d-2)}{2(d-1)}(3k_{1}.k_{2}+2k_{1}^{2}+2k_{2}^{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle+\eta_{\nu\alpha}\Big\{-24\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\beta}+12(\ell_{\mu}k_{1\beta}+\ell_{\beta}k_{2\mu})-\frac{3(d-2)}{d-1}k_{1\beta}k_{2\mu}\Big\}\Big].

The second diagram reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4\kappa^{2}\sqrt{2}m^{2}}{f}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(62)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(k_{1}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\times\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\nu}(\ell-k_{1})_{\alpha}(\ell-k_{1})_{\beta}.

Now we use Feynman’s parametrization to combine the denominators in
V_{1}
and perform loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+x(k_{1}+k_{2})
in the above expression. Then the non-vanishing part of
V_{1}
under
\ell\leftrightarrow-\ell
symmetry takes the following form

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2}\kappa^{2}m^{2}}{6f}\ \int_{0}^{1}dx\Bigg[(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})\Big\{\Big(6-\frac{24}{d}\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(1,2;\Delta\right)

(63)

\displaystyle+\Big(6m^{2}-6x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}+\frac{3(d-2)}{2(d-1)}(3k_{1}.k_{2}+2k_{1}^{2}+2k_{2}^{2})\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)\Big\}

\displaystyle-(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\ \Big(\frac{3(d-2)}{(d-1)}-24x(1-x)\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)\Bigg],

where
\Delta=m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}
.

Analogously using Feynman’s parametrization to combine the denominators in
V_{2}
and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+k_{1}(y+z)+k_{2}y
the non-vanishing part of
V_{2}
under
\ell\leftrightarrow-\ell
symmetry is

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4\kappa^{2}\sqrt{2}m^{2}}{f}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydz\ 2\delta(x+y+z-1)\Big[(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})\frac{2}{d(d+2)}\ \mathbf{J}\left(2,3;\Delta\right)

(64)

\displaystyle-(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\frac{4xy}{d}\ \mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)+(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})y^{2}x^{2}\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big]\ ,

where
\Delta=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2}+y(1-y)k_{2}^{2}+2xyk_{1}.k_{2}
.

In order to get the final answer for the vertex, we plug (
63
) and (
64
) into (
60
). We then set
d=4-\epsilon
and expand around
\epsilon=0
. Having done that, we expand the expression in powers of momenta keeping only terms containing four powers of momenta (this is the low-energy expansion). Performing the integrals over the Feynman parameters we obtain

V_{(hh\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=\\
f_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})+f_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})+f_{3}(k_{1},k_{2})\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2}),

(65)

where the functions
f_{i}(k_{1},k_{2})
read as

\displaystyle f_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{1440\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}f}\left(2(k_{1}^{2})^{2}+2(k_{2}^{2})^{2}+10(k_{1}.k_{2})^{2}+7k_{1}.k_{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})+3k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}\right),

(66)

\displaystyle f_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=+\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{360\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}f},

\displaystyle f_{3}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{720\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}f}\left(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+6(k_{1}.k_{2})\right).

Comparing (
66
) with (
36
) we conclude that

\Delta a=\frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad\Delta c=3\Delta a,\qquad r_{1}=\frac{\Delta a}{6}.

(67)

This is again in a perfect agreement with the first line in (
46
).

4.1.3
Graviton-Graviton-Dilaton-Dilaton Vertex

The simplest four-point vertex is the four dilaton vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
. It was already studied in
Komargodski:2011vj
, see also
Karateev:2022jdb
for a detailed analysis of this vertex in the free massive theory. We will not consider it here again. Instead, let us focus on the vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
. In our free massive theory this vertex is given by the sum of the following eight diagrams

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{4}
k_{3}
\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{4}
k_{3}
k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell
\ell-k_{3}
\ell

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}
k_{1}-\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell
\ell

\ell
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{4}
k_{3}

\ell
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\ell
k_{1}
k_{4}
k_{2}
k_{3}

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}
\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-\ell

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{4}
k_{3}
\ell
k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{4}-\ell

Let us denote the eight diagrams appearing here by
\mathcal{A}_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,8
. The order is counted from left to right and from top to bottom. Using Feynman’s rules the expression of the first diagram becomes

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{2}\kappa^{2}}{6f^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(68)

\displaystyle\Big[\eta_{\mu\alpha}\eta_{\nu\beta}\Big\{6(\ell^{2}+m^{2})-6\ell.(k_{1}+k_{2})+\frac{9(d-2)}{2(d-1)}k_{1}.k_{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle+\eta_{\nu\alpha}\Big\{-24\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\beta}+12(\ell_{\mu}k_{1\beta}+\ell_{\beta}k_{2\mu})-\frac{3(d-2)}{d-1}k_{1\beta}k_{2\mu}\Big\}\Big].

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+x(k_{1}+k_{2})
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{2}\kappa^{2}}{6f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \Big[(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\Big(6-\frac{24}{d}\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(1,2;\Delta\right)

(69)

\displaystyle+\Big(6m^{2}-s\Big(6x^{2}-6x+\frac{9(d-2)}{4(d-1)}\Big)\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)

\displaystyle-(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\Big(\frac{3(d-2)}{(d-1)}-24x+24x^{2}\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)\Big],

where
\Delta=m^{2}-sx(1-x)
.
The expression for the second diagram is

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(70)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\Big[\eta_{\mu\alpha}\eta_{\nu\beta}\Big\{6(\ell^{2}+m^{2})-6\ell.(k_{1}+k_{2})+\frac{9(d-2)}{2(d-1)}k_{1}.k_{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle+\eta_{\nu\alpha}\Big\{-24\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\beta}+12(\ell_{\mu}k_{1\beta}+\ell_{\beta}k_{2\mu})-\frac{3(d-2)}{d-1}k_{1\beta}k_{2\mu}\Big\}\Big].

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+y(k_{1}+k_{2})+zk_{3}
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydz\ 2\delta(x+y+z-1)\Big[

(71)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\Big\{\left(6-\frac{24}{d}\right)\mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)+\Big(6m^{2}+6sxy+3sz-\frac{9(d-2)}{4(d-1)}s\Big)\mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big\}

\displaystyle+\Big\{(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\Big(24y(1-y)-\frac{3(d-2)}{(d-1)}\Big)+12(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})z(1-2y)

\displaystyle+12(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})z(1-2y)-24z^{2}(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})\Big\}\mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big],

where
\Delta=m^{2}-sxy
.
The expression for the third diagram is

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4m^{2}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{1}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(72)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\nu}(\ell-k_{1})_{\alpha}(\ell-k_{1})_{\beta}.

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+k_{1}(y+z)+k_{2}y
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4m^{2}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydz\ 2\delta(x+y+z-1)\Big[\frac{2}{d(d+2)}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\mathbf{J}\left(2,3;\Delta\right)

(73)

\displaystyle-\frac{4xy}{d}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)+y^{2}x^{2}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big],

where
\Delta=m^{2}-sxy
.
Using Feynman rules the expression of the fourth diagram becomes

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(74)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{(k_{1}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\times\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\nu}(\ell-k_{1})_{\alpha}(\ell-k_{1})_{\beta}.

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+k_{1}(y+z)+k_{2}y+k_{3}w
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydzdw\ 6\delta(x+y+z+w-1)\Big[\frac{2}{d(d+2)}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\mathbf{J}\left(2,4;\Delta\right)

(75)

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{d}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2,\mu}^{\ \beta}(k_{2})(k_{2\nu}y+k_{3\nu}w)(k_{1\beta}(y+z-1)+k_{3\beta}w)\mathbf{J}\left(1,4;\Delta\right)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})(k_{2\mu}y+k_{3\mu}w)(k_{2\nu}y+k_{3\nu}w)(k_{1\alpha}(y+z-1)+k_{3\alpha}w)(k_{1\beta}(y+z-1)

\displaystyle+k_{3\beta}w)\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,4;\Delta\right)\Big],

where
\Delta=m^{2}-sxy-tzw
.
The contribution of the fifth diagram
\mathcal{A}_{5}
can be read off from the expression of
\mathcal{A}_{4}
above just after exchanging
k_{3}\leftrightarrow k_{4}
.

Using Feynman rules the expression of the sixth diagram becomes

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{3}-k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(76)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(k_{1}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\times\ell_{\mu}\ell_{\nu}(\ell-k_{3})_{\alpha}(\ell-k_{3})_{\beta}.

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+k_{1}z-k_{2}y+k_{3}(y+w)
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydzdw\ 6\delta(x+y+z+w-1)\Big[\frac{2}{d(d+2)}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\mathbf{J}\left(2,4;\Delta\right)

(77)

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{d}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2,\mu}^{\ \beta}(k_{2})(-k_{2\nu}y+k_{3\nu}(y+w))(k_{1\beta}z+k_{3\beta}(y+w-1))\mathbf{J}\left(1,4;\Delta\right)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}(k_{2})(-k_{2\mu}y+k_{3\mu}(y+w))(-k_{2\nu}y+k_{3\nu}(y+w))(k_{1\alpha}z+k_{3\alpha}(y+w-1))

\displaystyle\times(k_{1\beta}z+k_{3\beta}(y+w-1))\mathbf{J}\left(0,4;\Delta\right)\Big],

where
\Delta=m^{2}-uxy-tzw
.
The expression for the seventh diagram is

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(k_{4}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2m^{4}\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,2;m^{2}\right).

The contribution of the eighth diagram
\mathcal{A}_{8}
can be read off from the expression of
\mathcal{A}_{7}
above just after exchanging
k_{3}\leftrightarrow k_{4}
.

Now we sum the contributions of all eight diagrams and expand around
\epsilon=0
after setting
d=4-\epsilon
. Having done that, we expand the expression in powers of momenta keeping only terms up to fourth power in momenta. Performing the integrals over the Feynman parameters we obtain
(
44
) with the functions
g_{i}(s,t,u)
given by

\displaystyle g_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-\frac{3m^{4}}{4\pi^{2}\epsilon}+\frac{15m^{2}s+360\gamma m^{4}+360m^{4}\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}\right)}{960\pi^{2}}+\frac{2s^{2}+st+t^{2}}{960\pi^{2}}\right),

(79)

\displaystyle g_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-\frac{5m^{2}}{240\pi^{2}}+\frac{s}{240\pi^{2}}\right),\qquad g_{3}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(\frac{m^{4}}{8\pi^{2}}-\frac{u}{240\pi^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle g_{4}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}-\frac{s}{240\pi^{2}}\right),\qquad\quad g_{5}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{240\pi^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle g_{6}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{60\pi^{2}}\right),\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad g_{7}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{120\pi^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle g_{8}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{40\pi^{2}}\right),\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad g_{9}(s,t,u)=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{40\pi^{2}}\right).

Comparing the above expression with (
45
) we get
9
9

9

We do not match the cosmological constant
\lambda
because the free scalar theory has an extra anomaly that affects this term. We discuss this in detail in section
6
.

\Delta a=\frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad\Delta c=3\Delta a,\qquad r_{0}=-10\Delta a,\qquad r_{1}=\frac{\Delta a}{6}.

(80)

This is also consistent with the result (
67
) obtained from the three-point vertex
V_{(hh\varphi)}
.
This example nicely shows how the
low-energy
expansion is sensitive to both the
a
and
c
ultraviolet trace anomalies, which is the hallmark of anomaly matching.

4.2
Free Massive Dirac Fermion

In this section we use
a,b,c,...
as tangent space indices and
\mu,\nu,\rho,...
as curved space indices in
d
dimensional curved background.
With this convention, the massless Dirac field action in a curved background reads

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\text{free fermion}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\ \overline{\Psi}(x)\big(i\gamma^{a}E_{a}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\big)\Psi(x),

(81)

where
\overline{\Psi}(x)=\Psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
,
D_{\mu}\Psi=\partial_{\mu}\Psi+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}^{bc}\Sigma_{bc}\Psi
with the following form of Lorentz generator and spin connection

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Sigma_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}[\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}],

(82)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{bc}e_{\nu}^{\ a}\partial_{\mu}E_{c}^{\ \nu}+\eta^{bc}e_{\nu}^{\ a}\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}E_{c}^{\ \rho}.

(83)

Gamma matrices satisfies the following properties:
\gamma^{a\dagger}=\gamma^{0}\gamma^{a}\gamma^{0}
,
\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\}=-2\eta^{ab}\mathbb{I}
.
The relations between Vielbeins and metric are

\displaystyle\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=e_{\mu}^{\ a}e_{\nu}^{\ b}\eta_{ab},\qquad g^{\mu\nu}=E_{a}^{\ \mu}E_{b}^{\ \nu}\eta^{ab}.

(84)

Traces of gamma matrices

For performing computations we will need to evaluate traces of various products of gamma matrices. The basic relations read as

\displaystyle\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{1}}\gamma^{a_{2}}\cdots\gamma^{a_{2n+1}}]

\displaystyle=0\ \text{for}\ n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

(85)

\displaystyle\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{1}}\gamma^{a_{2}}]

\displaystyle=-d\eta^{a_{1}a_{2}},

\displaystyle\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{1}}\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{4}}]

\displaystyle=d(\eta^{a_{1}a_{2}}\eta^{a_{3}a_{4}}-\eta^{a_{1}a_{3}}\eta^{a_{2}a_{4}}+\eta^{a_{1}a_{4}}\eta^{a_{2}a_{3}}).

In
d=4
dimensions the last relation would contain the Levi-Civita symbol. This term however will not contribute in parity preserving theories (as the ones considered in this draft). Traces with higher number of gamma matrices can be evaluated using the above relations, anti-commutation relations and the cyclicity of trace. For example, the trace of six gamma matrices can be written as

\displaystyle\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{1}}\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{4}}\gamma^{a_{5}}\gamma^{a_{6}}]=

\displaystyle-\eta^{a_{1}a_{2}}\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{4}}\gamma^{a_{5}}\gamma^{a_{6}}]+\eta^{a_{1}a_{3}}\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{4}}\gamma^{a_{5}}\gamma^{a_{6}}]

(86)

\displaystyle-\eta^{a_{1}a_{4}}\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{5}}\gamma^{a_{6}}]+\eta^{a_{1}a_{5}}\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{4}}\gamma^{a_{6}}]

\displaystyle-\eta^{a_{1}a_{6}}\textbf{Tr}[\gamma^{a_{2}}\gamma^{a_{3}}\gamma^{a_{4}}\gamma^{a_{5}}].

Weyl invariance

Under a Weyl transformation with parameter
\sigma(x)

\displaystyle E_{a}^{\ \mu}(x)\rightarrow e^{-\sigma(x)}E_{a}^{\ \mu}(x),

(87)

\displaystyle\Psi(x)\rightarrow e^{-\big(\frac{d-1}{2}\big)\sigma(x)}\Psi(x),

(88)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\rightarrow\omega_{\mu}^{ab}+\big(\eta^{bc}e_{\mu}^{\ a}-\eta^{ac}e_{\mu}^{\ b}\big)E_{c}^{\ \rho}\partial_{\rho}\sigma\ .

(89)

Substituting the above transformation relations in the Dirac action (
81
) we get the following Weyl transformation property

\displaystyle A_{\text{free fermion}}[\Psi]\rightarrow A_{\text{free fermion}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}\int d^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\ \overline{\Psi}(x)E_{a}^{\ \mu}\Big[-(d-1)\gamma^{a}+(\gamma^{c}\eta^{ba}-\gamma^{b}\eta^{ca})\Sigma_{cb}\Big]\Psi(x)\partial_{\mu}\sigma(x).

(90)

Now using the definition of
\Sigma_{cb}
given above and anti-commutation relations of gamma matrices we find
(\gamma^{c}\eta^{ba}-\gamma^{b}\eta^{ca})\Sigma_{cb}=(d-1)\gamma^{a}
. This implies that the massless Dirac action in general dimension is Weyl invariant, of course.

Compensated action

Let us now deform the massless free fermion action by a mass term and compensate with dilaton we get the following QFT action of interest

\displaystyle\displaystyle A^{\text{compensated}}_{\text{free fermion}}[\Psi,\varphi,h]=\int d^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\ \overline{\Psi}(x)\Big(i\gamma^{a}E_{a}^{\mu}D_{\mu}-m\ e^{-\tau(x)}\Big)\Psi(x).

(91)

Let us now evaluate the Feynman rules for the action (
91
).
We will denote massive fermions by solid lines with charge arrows, dilatons by dashed lines and gravitons by wavy lines.
The Feynman rules for a massive Dirac fermion propagator and non-vanishing interaction vertices are given below

\beta
\alpha
p

\displaystyle=\frac{i(\not{p}-m\mathbb{I})_{\alpha\beta}}{p^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\ ,

\beta
\alpha
\varepsilon
p_{1}
p_{2}
k

\displaystyle=i\kappa\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\ \textbf{Sym}_{(\mu\nu)}\Big[\gamma^{\mu}p_{1}^{\nu}+\gamma^{\mu}k_{\rho}\Sigma^{\rho\nu}\Big]_{\alpha\beta}\ ,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\alpha}(k_{1}-k_{2})_{\alpha}\eta^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{\nu\sigma}-\gamma^{\mu}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(k_{1}+k_{2})_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha\rho}-\gamma^{\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(k_{1}+k_{2})_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha\mu}
