4.2.1
Dilaton-Dilaton-Dilaton Vertex

Here we focus on computing the three point vertices
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
and
V_{(hh\varphi)}
for our free fermion example.
In our convention all the dilatons are incoming and with arbitrary momenta. The vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
is given by the sum of the following two Feynman diagrams

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
\ell
\ell+k_{3}
\ell-k_{1}

k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}
\ell
\ell+k_{2}
\ell-k_{1}

Let us denote these diagrams by
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
respectively. Then

V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=W_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})+W_{2}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}).

(92)

Using the Feynman rules one can write the above Feynman diagrams in an algebraic form. The first diagram reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{3}}{2\sqrt{2}f^{3}}\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\textbf{Tr}\big[(\not{\ell}-\not{k}_{1}-m\mathbb{I})(\not{\ell}+\not{k}_{3}-m\mathbb{I})(\not{\ell}-m\mathbb{I})\big]}{[\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon][(\ell+k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon][(\ell-k_{1})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon]}.

(93)

Evaluating the trace of gamma matrices and replacing
k_{3}=-k_{1}-k_{2}
the above expression simplifies to

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{dm^{4}}{2\sqrt{2}f^{3}}\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{(-3\ell^{2}+2k_{2}.\ell+4k_{1}.\ell-k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}.k_{2}+m^{2})}{[\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon][(k_{1}+k_{2}-\ell)^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon][(\ell-k_{1})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon]}.

(94)

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators and doing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+(y+z)k_{1}+yk_{2}
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{dm^{4}}{2\sqrt{2}f^{3}}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydz\ 2\delta(x+y+z-1)\Big[-3\ \mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)+\Big(k_{1}.k_{2}(6xy+2z-1)

(95)

\displaystyle+k_{1}^{2}x(3y+3z-1)+k_{2}^{2}y(2-3y)+m^{2}\Big)\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big]\ ,

where
\Delta=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2}+y(1-y)k_{2}^{2}+2xyk_{1}.k_{2}
. The contribution of the second diagram
W_{2}
can be read off from the above expression
W_{1}
just by exchanging
k_{2}\leftrightarrow k_{3}
.

Now let us sum over
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
and then set
d=4-\epsilon
and expand around
\epsilon=0
. Having done that, we expand the expression in powers of momenta keeping only terms containing four powers of momenta. Performing the integrals over the Feynman parameters we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2880\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}f^{3}}\Big(11\left((k_{1}^{2})^{2}+(k_{2}^{2})^{2}+(k_{3}^{2})^{2}\right)+14\left(k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}k_{1}^{2}\right)\Big).

Comparing the above expression with (
30
) we get,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta a\ =\ \frac{11}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad r_{1}\ =\ \frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}}.

(97)

The value of
\Delta a
perfectly agrees with (
46
).

4.2.2
Graviton-Graviton-Dilaton Vertex

The vertex
V_{(hh\varphi)}
is given by the sum of the following three diagrams

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
\ell
\ell-k_{3}

k_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
\ell
\ell+k_{2}
\ell-k_{3}

k_{3}
k_{2}
k_{1}
\ell
\ell+k_{1}
\ell-k_{3}

Let us denote these diagrams by
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
respectively. Then

V_{(hh\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=U_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})+U_{2}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})+U_{3}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}).

(98)

Using Feynman’s rules the algebraic form of the first diagram becomes

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa^{2}m}{\sqrt{2}f}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{\rho\sigma}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(99)

\displaystyle\textbf{Tr}\Big[\Big(2\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(\not{\ell}+m\mathbb{I})-\frac{3}{2}(\eta^{\nu\rho}\gamma^{\mu}\ell^{\sigma}+\eta^{\mu\sigma}\gamma^{\rho}\ell^{\nu})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\alpha}(k_{1}-k_{2})_{\alpha}\eta^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{\nu\sigma}-\gamma^{\mu}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(k_{1}+k_{2})_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha\rho}-\gamma^{\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(k_{1}+k_{2})_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha\mu}

\displaystyle-\gamma^{\rho}k_{2}^{\nu}\Sigma^{\mu\sigma}-\gamma^{\mu}k_{1}^{\sigma}\Sigma^{\rho\nu}\Big)(\not{\ell}-m\mathbb{I})(\not{\ell}-\not{k}_{3}-m\mathbb{I})\Big].

Evaluating the trace of gamma matrices the above expression reduces to

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{dm^{2}\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{\rho\sigma}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(100)

\displaystyle\Big[\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(2(\ell^{2}+m^{2})-2\ell.k_{3}+k_{3}^{2})-\frac{3}{2}\eta^{\mu\rho}\big\{(2\ell+k_{2})^{\nu}(2\ell+k_{1})^{\sigma}\big\}\Big].

Now using Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators, doing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell+xk_{3}
and keeping the non-vanishing terms under
\ell\leftrightarrow-\ell
symmetry, we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{dm^{2}\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \Bigg[(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\Bigg\{\left(2-\frac{6}{d}\right)\mathbf{J}\left(1,2;\Delta\right)

(101)

\displaystyle+\left((1-2x+2x^{2})(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}+2m^{2}\right)\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\ (2x-1)^{2}\mathbf{J}\left(0,2;\Delta\right)\Bigg],

where
\Delta=m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}
.
The second diagram evaluates to

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}m}{\sqrt{2}f}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}(k_{1})\varepsilon_{\rho\sigma}(k_{2})\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(\ell-k_{3})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}

(102)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell+k_{2})^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}\textbf{Tr}\Big[(\not{\ell}-\not{k}_{3}-m)\big(\gamma^{\mu}(\ell+k_{2})^{\nu}+\gamma^{\mu}k_{1\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha\nu}\big)

\displaystyle(\not{\ell}+\not{k}_{2}-m)\big(\gamma^{\rho}\ell^{\sigma}+\gamma^{\rho}k_{2\beta}\Sigma^{\beta\sigma}\big)(\not{\ell}-m)\Big]

Now evaluating the gamma matrix traces, combining the denominators using Feynman parametrization and performing loop momentum shift
\ell\rightarrow\ell-xk_{1}-(x+y)k_{2}
the non-vanishing contribution from the above expression becomes

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}m^{2}}{\sqrt{2}f}\int_{0}^{1}dxdydz\ 2\delta(x+y+z-1)\Bigg[(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\Bigg\{\frac{d-6}{d+2}\ \mathbf{J}\left(2,3;\Delta\right)

(103)

\displaystyle+\left(m^{2}+x^{2}k_{1}^{2}+(1-2xz)k_{1}.k_{2}+z^{2}k_{2}^{2}\right)\mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\Big\{\left(-1+(12-d)xz\right)\ \mathbf{J}\left(1,3;\Delta\right)-dxz\big(m^{2}+x^{2}k_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(1-2xz\right)k_{1}.k_{2}+z^{2}k_{2}^{2}\big)\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Big\}

\displaystyle+(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\ dxz(1-4xz)\ \mathbf{J}\left(0,3;\Delta\right)\Bigg]\ ,

where
\Delta=m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2}+z(1-z)k_{2}^{2}+2xzk_{1}.k_{2}
.
The contribution of the third Feynman diagram
U_{3}
can be read off from the above expression of
U_{2}
by exchanging
(\varepsilon_{1},k_{1})\leftrightarrow(\varepsilon_{2},k_{2})
.

Now let us sum over the contributions of the three diagrams then set
d=4-\epsilon
and expand around
\epsilon=0
. Having done that, we expand the expression in powers of momenta keeping only terms containing four powers of momenta. Performing the integrals over the Feynman parameters we obtain

\displaystyle V_{(hh\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})

(104)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{720\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}f}\Big[(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\left\{14k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}+6\left((k_{1}^{2})^{2}+(k_{2}^{2})^{2}\right)+25(k_{1}.k_{2})^{2}+21k_{1}.k_{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})\right\}

\displaystyle-(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\ \left\{6(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})+26k_{1}.k_{2}\right\}+7(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\Big]+\cdots

Comparing the above expression with (
36
) we conclude that

\Delta a=\frac{11}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad\Delta c=\frac{18}{5760\pi^{2}},\qquad r_{1}=\frac{1}{5760\pi^{2}}.

(105)

The values of
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
perfectly agree with (
46
).

5
The Dilaton-Graviton Scattering Amplitude

In this section we promote our background fields to dynamical fields by giving them kinetic terms. A convenient choice for these kinetic terms is

\displaystyle A^{\varphi}_{\text{kinetic}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bar{f}^{2}}{6}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-\widehat{g}}\ \widehat{R},\qquad-\frac{\bar{f}^{2}}{6}\equiv-\frac{f^{2}}{6}-M^{2}r_{0},

(106)

\displaystyle A^{h}_{\text{kinetic}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\bar{\kappa}^{2}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ R,\qquad\;\;\,\frac{1}{2\bar{\kappa}^{2}}\equiv\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}+\frac{f^{2}}{6}.

(107)

These kinetic terms preserve diffeomorphism invariance. The kinetic term of the dilaton also classically preserves Weyl invariance. Instead, the kinetic term of the graviton breaks Weyl invariance at the Planck scale
\bar{\kappa}^{-1}
.
The IR action describing our dynamical background fields is now given by
10
10

10

For a recent discussion on coupling of the dynamical gravity to classically scale invariant QFTs see
Gabadadze:2023quw
.

A=A_{\text{EFT}}+A^{\varphi}_{\text{kinetic}}+A^{h}_{\text{kinetic}},

(108)

where the effective action is given by (
17
).

Let us write the dilaton kinetic term in terms of the
\varphi(x)
fields explicitly

A^{\varphi}_{\text{kinetic}}=-M^{2}r_{0}\int d^{4}x\ \sqrt{-\widehat{g}}\widehat{R}\\
+\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ \left[-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-\frac{f^{2}}{6}R+\frac{\sqrt{2}f}{6}R\varphi-\frac{1}{12}R\varphi^{2}\right]

(109)

and make several comments.
The shifted coupling constant
\bar{\kappa}
is used in the definition (
107
) in order to effectively remove the
-\frac{f^{2}}{6}R
term in (
109
) once it is summed up with the graviton kinetic term in the combined action (
108
). The extra term proportional to
r_{0}M^{2}
is introduced in (
106
) in order to precisely cancel the
r_{0}M^{2}
term in the invariant part of the effective action (
15
). This is done in order to slightly simplify the expressions in this section. It allows us to effectively set
r_{0}=0
in all the equations.
In order to perform computations from now on we also set the IR cosmological constant in the effective action (
15
) to zero, namely
\lambda=0
. This can be achieved by appropriately choosing the form of (
9
).

In this paper we focus for concreteness on the case of QFTs with explicit UV conformal symmetry breaking which undergo a renormalization group flow. Our results, however, also apply to the case of spontaneous symmetry breaking. Let us briefly explain why. In the case of spontaneous symmetry breaking the dilaton is a physical particle and not simply a probe. It is a Goldstone boson of the spontaneous conformal symmetry breaking. Its kinetic terms is generated automatically and needs not to be added by hand. More precisely the kinetic term for the physical dilaton is given by the
r_{0}M^{2}\widehat{R}
term in the effective action (
15
). The
r_{0}M^{2}
is associated to the scale of the spontaneous conformal symmetry breaking
f
as
r_{0}M^{2}=-\frac{f^{2}}{6}
. As a result the parameter
r_{0}
effectively disappears from the discussion. Also, in the spontaneous symmetry breaking case
\lambda
is automatically zero. Finally, the difference of the UV and IR trace-anomalies should be interpreted according to the footnote
8
.

Now using the action (
108
) we can compute the graviton-dilaton scattering amplitude

\mathcal{T}_{h\varphi\rightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3}).

(110)

We require that our dilatons and gravitons are weakly coupled – this is enforced by the “decoupling” limit

\kappa\rightarrow 0,\qquad f\rightarrow\infty,\qquad\kappa\ll\frac{1}{f}.

(111)

The above limit means in practice that all the expressions should be first expanded in small
\kappa
keeping
f
finite and subsequently expanded in small
1/f
. This limit also makes physical sense, as we expect the dilaton decay constant
f
(if a dynamical dilaton exists in nature) to be not larger than the Planck scale
\bar{\kappa}^{-1}
.

One can foresee that the scattering amplitude computed using the action (
108
) at the leading order
\kappa^{2}
will contain “trivial” terms with no
f
dependence which come from the kinetic terms (
106
) and (
107
). The non-trivial dependence on
\Delta a
and
\Delta c
will be uncovered instead at the order
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
.
Let us also recall the definition of the Mandelstam variables for the scattering amplitude (
110
)

s\equiv-(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2},\qquad t\equiv-(k_{1}-k_{3})^{2},\qquad u\equiv-(k_{1}-k_{4})^{2}.

(112)

Since both the graviton and the dilaton particles are massless, we have the following constraint

s+t+u=0.

(113)

5.1
Propagators and Additional Vertices

In this subsection we summarize propagators and vertices following from the action (
108
). As argued above, from now on, we set
r_{0}=0
and
\lambda=0
in (
106
), (
107
), and (
108
) for the computation below.

Two-point vertices and propagators

Let us begin by examining the propagators and 2-point vertices in the theory described by the action (
108
). To do this, we expand the action (
108
) up to quadratic order in the graviton-dilaton fields. The vertices and propagators resulting from the sum of the kinetic terms (
106
) and (
107
) will be denoted by
U
and
\Delta
respectively. The vertices generated from the EFT action (
17
) will be referred to as blob vertices and denoted by
V
.

The part of the action (
108
) which contains two dilatons reads as

A=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\partial_{\mu}\varphi(x)\partial^{\mu}\varphi(x)+\frac{1}{f^{2}}\int d^{4}x\left(18r_{1}\partial^{2}\varphi\partial^{2}\varphi+\ldots\right),

(114)

where the ellipses above represents the remaining terms at order
O(f^{-2})
which contain higher than four power of derivatives of the dilaton field. These terms will always be ignored from now on. The first term of the above action will be used to derive the propagator for the dilaton filed and the order
f^{-2}
part of the action will be treated as an EFT vertex denoted by the blob. The expressions of the dilaton propagator and the two-dilaton blob vertex read

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\varphi)}(p)=\hbox to43.08pt{\vbox to21.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=O(\kappa f^{-1}).

(119)

Note that since the graviton-dilaton vertex
U_{(h\varphi)}
is proportional to
\eta_{\mu\nu}
any Lorentz contraction of this vertex with the on-shell graviton polarization tensor gives zero. As a consequence, any Feynman diagram where the dilaton is converted to the external graviton using
U_{(h\varphi)}
vanishes on-shell after contraction with the graviton polarization tensor. Therefore, we do not consider such Feynman diagrams. We have also explicitly verified that Feynman diagrams involving vertices
V_{(h\varphi)}
contribute to a power always higher than
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
in the decoupling limit (
111
). This is the reason we have not explicitly evaluated (
119
).

The graviton propagator at the leading order in
\kappa
simply follows from the sum of the kinetic terms (
106
) and (
107
). Since it is the standard result we do not derive it here and simply quote the final answer

\displaystyle\Delta^{(h)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2},\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\eta_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\eta_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}+\eta_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\eta_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}-\eta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\eta_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\Big)\frac{-i}{p^{2}-i\epsilon}.

(120)

The two-graviton blob vertex that follows from (
17
) turns out to be at order
\kappa^{2}
, and it does not contribute to the amplitude (
110
) up to the order
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
. Therefore, we are not presenting it here.

Three and four point vertices

The new vertices following from the sum of the kinetic terms (
106
) and (
107
) will be denoted by
U
, and the vertices generated from the EFT action (
17
) will be referred to as blob vertices and denoted by
V
. The total contribution to the interaction vertices will be the sum of
U
and
V
.

The graviton-dilaton-dilaton vertex which follows from (
106
) is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{(h\varphi\varphi)}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\mu\nu
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}

(121)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\kappa\left(k_{2}^{\mu}k_{3}^{\nu}+k_{2}^{\nu}k_{3}^{\mu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{2}.k_{3}\right)-\frac{i\kappa}{6}\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{1}^{2}.

The graviton-dilaton-dilaton vertex
V_{(h\varphi\varphi)}^{\mu\nu}
which follows from (
17
) was computed in (
32
) up to fourth power in momenta.

The graviton-graviton-dilaton vertex which follows from (
106
) is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{(hh\varphi)}^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}

(122)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\sqrt{2}f\kappa^{2}}{6}\Big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho\sigma}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})-4\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})-6\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}k_{1}.k_{2}

\displaystyle+2(k_{1}^{\rho}k_{2}^{\mu}\eta^{\nu\sigma}+k_{1}^{\sigma}k_{2}^{\nu}\eta^{\mu\rho})\Big).

The graviton-graviton-dilaton vertex
V_{(hh\varphi)}
which follows from (
17
) was computed in (
35
).

The three-graviton vertex which follows from the sum of (
106
) and (
107
) reads

\displaystyle U_{(hhh)\lambda\kappa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=\hbox to107.34pt{\vbox to73.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 58.69443pt\lower-36.98395pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(123)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2i\kappa\,\Big[-\frac{3}{2}k_{1}.k_{2}(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})\eta_{\lambda\kappa}+2k_{1}.k_{2}\varepsilon_{1\lambda\alpha}\varepsilon_{2\ \kappa}^{\alpha}+(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})k_{1\lambda}k_{1\kappa}+(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})k_{1\lambda}k_{2\kappa}

\displaystyle+(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2})k_{2\kappa}k_{2\lambda}+(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\varepsilon_{1\lambda\kappa}+(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})\varepsilon_{2\lambda\kappa}+(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{1})\eta_{\lambda\kappa}

\displaystyle-2k_{1\alpha}\varepsilon_{2}^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon_{1\beta\lambda}k_{1\kappa}-2k_{2\alpha}\varepsilon_{1}^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon_{2\beta\lambda}k_{2\kappa}-2k_{1}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{2\alpha\lambda}k_{2}^{\beta}\varepsilon_{1\beta\kappa}\Big].

On the other hand the three-graviton vertex
V_{(hhh)}
following from (
17
) appears at order
\kappa^{3}
, hence it does not contribute to the amplitude (
110
) at the
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
order.

No three-dilaton interaction term is present in (
106
) hence
U_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}=0
. On the other hand the three dilaton blob vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
has been computed in (
30
) and it is non-vanishing for off-shell dilatons.

The graviton-dilaton-graviton-dilaton vertex which follows from the sum of (
106
) and (
107
) reads

\displaystyle\displaystyle U_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})

\displaystyle=

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

(124)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\kappa^{2}\Big(\frac{t}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-4(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4})-4(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\Big).

The graviton-dilaton-graviton-dilaton vertex
V_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}
which follows from (
17
) was already computed in (
44
) up to the fourth power in momentum. We write its explicit expression here again. It reads

\displaystyle V_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})=\hbox to92.39pt{\vbox to80.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 46.19403pt\lower-40.0786pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(125)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{2}}\Big[\left(3\Delta c\,t^{2}+3\Delta a(s^{2}+u^{2})\right)\times(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-12(-\Delta a+\Delta c)t\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})

\displaystyle-24\Delta a\,u\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})+24\Delta a\,s\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})-24\Delta a\,t\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})

\displaystyle+12(-\Delta a+\Delta c)\times(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})+\left(24\Delta a+432r_{1}\right)\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})

\displaystyle+\left(24\Delta a+432r_{1}\right)\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})+48\Delta a\times(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})

\displaystyle+864r_{1}\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+864r_{1}\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})

\displaystyle-864r_{1}\times(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})\Big].

5.2
Graviton-Dilaton Amplitude

The leading order in
1/f
graviton-dilaton amplitude (
110
) is given by the sum of the following diagrams which contribute at order
\kappa^{2}
:

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{4}
k_{3}
k_{2}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}
k_{4}

These diagrams arise only from the kinetic terms and contain no information about the QFT to which the graviton and dilaton couple.
Evaluating these diagrams we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\text{leading in }1/f}_{h\varphi\rightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})

(126)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\kappa^{2}\left(\frac{(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4})}{s}+\frac{(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{4})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})}{u}\right)

\displaystyle+\kappa^{2}\left[t(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-4(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4}+k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{t}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})-4t(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4s(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})

\displaystyle-4u(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})

\displaystyle+8(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})\Big].

The sub-leading order in
1/f
of the amplitude is given by the sum of the following diagrams, which contribute at order
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
(additionally, we will consider only terms up to fourth order in momentum):

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{4}
k_{3}
k_{2}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{4}
k_{3}
k_{2}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{4}
k_{3}
k_{2}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}
k_{4}

\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{3}
k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}
k_{4}

We enumerate these diagram from left to right and then from top to bottom. With this convention let us compute them. We find that the first and the second diagrams are given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}-i\epsilon}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu\nu}V_{(h\varphi\varphi)\mu\nu}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{1}-k_{2})U^{\rho\sigma}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(-k_{3},k_{1}+k_{2},-k_{4})\varepsilon_{3\rho\sigma}

(127)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{144r_{1}i\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{2}.

The third diagram is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{-i}{(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}-i\epsilon}\right)^{2}\varepsilon_{1\mu\nu}U^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{1}-k_{2})V_{(\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1}+k_{2},-k_{1}-k_{2})

(128)

\displaystyle\times\ U^{\rho\sigma}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(-k_{3},k_{1}+k_{2},-k_{4})\varepsilon_{3\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\mathcal{T}_{2}.

Analogously for the fourth, fifth and sixth diagrams we find that

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{4}=i\mathcal{T}_{5}=-i\mathcal{T}_{6}

(129)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{144r_{1}i\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{4})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2}).

The seventh diagram is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U_{(hhh)}^{\mu\nu}(k_{1},-k_{3},-k_{1}+k_{3};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})\Delta^{(h)}_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}(k_{1}-k_{3})V_{(h\varphi\varphi)}^{\rho\sigma}(k_{1}-k_{3},k_{2},-k_{4})

(130)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2i\kappa^{2}\Delta a}{f^{2}}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})-4t(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4s(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})

\displaystyle-4u(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})+4(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})

\displaystyle+8(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})\Big].

The eighth diagram is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{8}=V_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3}).

(131)

The ninth diagram is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{9}=-\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{3}}\ \Delta a\ t\left(3t(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-4(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\right).

(132)

Finally, the tenth diagram is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{10}=\frac{i\kappa^{2}}{3f^{3}}\ \Delta a\ t\left(3t(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-4(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\right).

(133)

To summarize, the sub-leading in
1/f
graviton-dilaton amplitude reads as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\text{sub-leading in }1/f}_{h\varphi\longrightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})\ =\ \sum_{I=1}^{10}\mathcal{T}_{I}

(134)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\left(\Delta c-\Delta a\right)\times\Big[t^{2}(\varepsilon_{1}.\varepsilon_{3})-4t(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})+4(k_{1}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{1})(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\Big].

Combining (
126
) and (
134
) we obtain the final expression for the graviton-dilaton scattering amplitude

\mathcal{T}_{h\varphi\longrightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})=\mathcal{T}^{\text{leading in }1/f}_{h\varphi\longrightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})+\\
\mathcal{T}^{\text{sub-leading in }1/f}_{h\varphi\longrightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})+O(\kappa^{3}).

(135)

Let us remind that in the computation of the amplitude above, we set the cosmological constant (c.c.)
\lambda
to zero in the invariant part of the EFT action (
15
). This is done for the convenience of having scattering of massless particles and can always be achieved by tuning the UV c.c. appropriately. It is not necessary in general.
11
11

11

If we do not set
\lambda
to zero in (
15
), it effectively provides mass to the dilaton at order
\frac{M^{2}}{f}\sqrt{\lambda}
. Now, once we properly amputate the external dilatons in the computation of the scattering amplitude, the c.c. term only contributes to the amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(s,t,u)
in (
139
) at zeroth power in momenta and at order
\kappa^{2}f^{-2}
in the decoupling limit.
However, the physical content of what we are doing can be rephrased as sending a graviton through the state created by the energy-momentum tensor trace, and the massless dilaton is just a convenient choice for this purpose.

The result of the 2-graviton-2-dilaton amplitude in (
135
) is not affected by the potential presence of interactions between the IR CFT operators and gravitons/dilatons as we show in appendix
B
.

Linearized gauge invariance

The amplitude (
135
) is invariant under linearized gauge transformations

\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}_{i}(k_{i})\rightarrow\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}_{i}(k_{i})+\chi^{\mu}k_{i}^{\nu}\ +\ \chi^{\nu}k_{i}^{\mu},

(136)

where
\chi^{\mu}(x)
is some generic vector field with
k_{i\mu}\chi^{\mu}
=0. Consider the following object

(H_{i})^{\mu\nu,\,\rho\sigma}\equiv k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\rho}\varepsilon_{i}^{\nu\sigma}(k_{i})-k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\sigma}\varepsilon_{i}^{\nu\rho}(k_{i})-k_{i}^{\nu}k_{i}^{\rho}\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu\sigma}(k_{i})+k_{i}^{\nu}k_{i}^{\sigma}\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu\rho}(k_{i}).

(137)

It is explicitly invariant under the transformation (
136
). Gauge invariant amplitudes should be built only as invariant contractions of (
137
). We can define the following two tensor structures

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv(H_{1})^{\mu\nu,\,\rho\sigma}(H_{3})_{\mu\nu,\,\rho\sigma},

(138)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv(H_{1})^{\mu\nu,\,\rho\sigma}(H_{3})_{\mu\nu,\,\rho\sigma}+\left(\frac{16}{su}\right)\,k_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}k_{3}^{\alpha_{2}}(H_{1})^{\mu\nu}{}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}k_{1}^{\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{\beta_{2}}(H_{3})_{\mu\nu,\,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{8}{su}\right)^{2}\,\left(k_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}k_{3}^{\alpha_{2}}k_{2}^{\alpha_{3}}k_{3}^{\alpha_{4}}(H_{1})_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\,\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\right)\left(k_{1}^{\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{\beta_{2}}k_{1}^{\beta_{3}}k_{2}^{\beta_{4}}(H_{3})_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2},\,\beta_{3}\beta_{4}}\right).

More generally the construction of tensor structures in
d
dimensions was recently discussed in
Chowdhury:2019kaq
.
It is straightforward to check that the expression (
135
) can be rewritten in the following simple form

\mathcal{T}_{h\varphi\longrightarrow h\varphi}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})=\kappa^{2}\frac{su}{t^{3}}\,\mathbf{T}_{2}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\left(\Delta c-\Delta a\right)\mathbf{T}_{1}.

(139)

We use the centre of mass frame approach recently reviewed in
Hebbar:2020ukp
and applied to spin one massless particles (a.k.a photons) in
Haring:2022sdp
.
Using the explicit expressions for graviton polarizations given in appendix
C.2
we can evaluate the graviton-dilaton amplitude in the center of mass frame. It reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{-2}^{-2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\,\frac{su}{t},

(140)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{-2}^{+2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\left(\Delta c-\Delta a\right)t^{2}.

We see that, miraculously, the helicity flipping amplitude is universally proportional to
\Delta c-\Delta a
at low energies.
More formally, we can read off
(\Delta c-\Delta a)
from taking the second derivative in
t
and then the following limit

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\Delta c-\Delta a)=\lim_{s,t\to 0}\lim_{f\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}\frac{f^{2}}{2\kappa^{2}}\ \partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,-s-t).

(141)

In appendix
C
we discuss some simple properties of
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,-s-t)
. First of all, we show that this amplitude vanishes in the forward limit,
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)=0
. This is why the amplitude looks like
t^{2}
at low energies and does not have a piece
s^{2}+u^{2}
. Second, we write a dispersion relation

\Delta c-\Delta a={f^{2}\over\kappa^{2}}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{\pi}\frac{\text{Im}\,\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{s},

(142)

where
m
is the mass gap which represents the mass of the lowest massive particle integrated out along the RG flow (in gapless theories
m=0
but
\text{Im}\,\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)
vanishes rapidly at small
s
since the dilaton is decoupled from the infrared CFT so effectively the sum rule does not probe the infrared CFT degrees of freedom but only the massive particles which are effectively responsible for the
\Delta c-\Delta a
in the RG flow).

6
Trace Anomalies in Coupling Space (Resonance Anomalies)

So far we have discussed QFTs coupled to the background fields
\Omega(x),\;g_{\mu\nu}(x)
and the
a
- and
c
-trace anomalies. These trace anomalies enter through (
7
) which does not contain
\Omega(x)
itself.
A more general class of trace anomalies arises in QFTs where the
ultraviolet
CFT has operators with integer (“resonant”) scaling dimension. These anomalies are sometimes called “resonance” or “coupling space” anomalies.
To be concrete and to avoid cluttered notation, consider a UV CFT which contains a scalar operator
\mathcal{O}(x)
whose scaling dimension is
\Delta=2
. We couple this CFT to the background (source) field
J(x)
in a conformally invariant way as

\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}J(x)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta=2}(x).

Notice that
J(x)
is now required to transform under Weyl transformations like
J(x)\rightarrow e^{-2\sigma(x)}J(x)
. One can show that the partition function of the CFT with the above deformation has the following Weyl anomaly
12
12

12

Similarly to the discussion after (
7
), the variation (
143
) is in a certain scheme. We could add local non-Weyl invariant terms in the UV of the form
\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}R(x)J(x)
which would affect the variation (
143
) by exact terms of the form
\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\sigma(x)\square J(x)
.
,
13
13

13

In order to do this one computes the connected functional
W[J,\,g_{\mu\nu}]
as a series expansion in
J(x)
. The quadratic term in
J(x)
in this expansion is UV divergent. In order to regularize it we are forced to introduce Weyl symmetry breaking counter terms which introduce the new anomaly.

\delta_{\sigma}W[J,\,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\sigma(x)\left(-a_{UV}E_{4}+c_{UV}\mathcal{W}^{2}+c^{\text{UV}}_{J}J^{2}\right).

(143)

Here the real coefficient
c_{J}^{UV}
is the new trace anomaly. It is clear why this new trace anomaly arises only for operators of special scaling dimensions: the variation of the connected functional
W[J,\,g_{\mu\nu}]
has to be local in the sources of the ultraviolet CFT. We will find that in the free massless scalar CFT with the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta=2}(x)
given by the square of the free field
\Phi(x)^{2}
, the value of the new trace anomaly is
c^{\text{UV}}_{J}=\tfrac{1}{8\pi^{2}}
.

Suppose now that the source field has the following special form
J(x)=\Omega(x)^{2}M^{2}
, where
M
is a mass parameter and
\Omega(x)\equiv e^{-\tau(x)}
denotes the dilaton. Such a choice of
J(x)
triggers an RG flow. Requiring that the IR effective action of this theory correctly reproduces the Weyl anomaly (
143
), we arrive at the following expression

A_{\text{EFT}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]=-a_{\text{UV}}\times A_{a}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]+c_{\text{UV}}\times A_{c}[\tau,\,g_{\mu\nu}]+c^{\text{UV}}_{J}\times A_{c_{J}}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]+A_{\text{invariant}}[\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}],

(144)

where the new term
A_{c_{J}}
in the effective action reads as

A_{c_{J}}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]=M^{4}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,\tau(x)\,e^{-4\tau(x)}.

(145)

One can use the effective action (
144
) to compute vertices of dilatons at the lowest order in momentum in order to extract the value of
c^{\text{UV}}_{J}
and the
\lambda
coefficient appearing in (
15
). We will show that by computing one- and two-point vertices of dilatons one can always disentangle the values of
c^{\text{UV}}_{J}
and
\lambda
.

In the previous paragraph the source
J(x)
itself triggered the RG flow. There could be another, distinct, situation when the RG flow is triggered by other operators. Assuming the IR theory is gapped, then, in order to match the new trace anomaly the IR effective action must contain instead

A_{c_{J}}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,\tau(x)J^{2}

(146)

multiplied by
c^{\text{UV}}_{J}
. One will also have more Weyl invariant terms compared to (
15
) such as for example

\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}J^{2},\qquad\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}e^{-2\tau}J,\qquad\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\widehat{R}e^{-2\tau}J,\qquad\ldots

In general,
A_{\rm invariant}
is a local coordinate invariant functional of
\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}
and
\widehat{J}=e^{2\tau}J
. Notice that the new terms involving
\widehat{J}
in the effective action
A_{\rm invariant}
do not give anything new if we set
J(x)=M^{2}\Omega(x)^{2}
. The only novelty comes from the term (
146
) as we will discuss below in the free scalar example.

Finally, if in the IR the theory is described by an IR CFT which contains operators with
\Delta=2
scaling dimensions which can couple to
J(x)
, it will contain an IR “coupling space” anomaly
c^{\text{IR}}_{J}
. In order to match the new anomaly one will have to multiply (
146
) by
\Delta c_{J}\equiv c_{J}^{\text{UV}}-c_{J}^{\text{IR}}
.

In this section we study the “coupling space” trace anomaly in a simple example. We start by considering a massless free boson on a curved background coupled to the external background (source) field
J(x)
. We show that this theory has the “coupling space” trace anomaly
c_{J}
and we compute it explicitly. We then focus on the case when
J(x)=-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\Omega(x)^{2}
, where
\Omega(x)
is the compensator field and
m
is the mass parameter. Such choice of
J(x)
triggers an RG flow. Notice, that this case precisely describes the compensated free massive scalar theory already studied in section
4.1
. We derive the IR effective action under the requirement that the “coupling space” anomaly is invariant along the flow (the anomaly should be matched in IR). We will test our IR effective action by performing an explicit perturbative computation.

Coupling space trace anomaly

Consider the following action

A[\Phi,J,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g(x)}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x)\partial_{\nu}\Phi(x)-\frac{1}{12}R(x)\Phi^{2}(x)+J(x)\Phi^{2}(x)\Big),

(147)

Using the action (
147
) let us compute the connected functional defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle e^{iW[J,g_{\mu\nu}]}\equiv\int[d\Phi]_{g}\ e^{iA[\Phi,J,g_{\mu\nu}]}.

(148)

One can rewrite this expression as

\displaystyle\displaystyle e^{iW[J,g_{\mu\nu}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{iW_{\text{free}}[g_{\mu\nu}]}\times e^{iW_{\text{source}}[J,g_{\mu\nu}]}+\ldots,

(149)

where the free connected functional is defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle e^{iW_{\text{free}}[J,g_{\mu\nu}]}\equiv\int[d\Phi]_{g}\ e^{\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g(x)}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x)\partial_{\nu}\Phi(x)-\frac{1}{12}R(x)\Phi^{2}(x)\Big)}

(150)

and the source connected functional is given by

W_{\text{source}}[J,g_{\mu\nu}]=-i\log\Big(1-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x_{1}\sqrt{-g(x_{1})}\int d^{4}x_{2}\sqrt{-g(x_{2})}J(x_{1})J(x_{2})\\
\times\langle\Phi^{2}(x_{1})\Phi^{2}(x_{2})\rangle^{g}_{\text{free scalar}}+O(J^{3})\Big).

(151)

The time-ordered correlation function is denoted by angular brackets.

Let us now focus on the flat background
g_{\mu\nu}(x)=\eta_{\mu\nu}
. Using the Wick contraction in (
151
) and focusing only on the quadratic term in
J(x)
we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\int d^{4}x_{1}\int d^{4}x_{2}J(x_{1})J(x_{2})\left(\langle\Phi(x_{1})\Phi(x_{2})\rangle_{\text{free scalar}}\right)^{2}.

(152)

The two-point function in the free massless theory is given by

\langle\Phi(x_{1})\Phi(x_{2})\rangle_{\text{free scalar}}=\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}e^{ip\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\frac{-i}{p^{2}-i\epsilon}.

(153)

Plugging this into (
152
) and performing the position space integrals we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\mu^{4-d}\int\frac{d^{d}q}{(2\pi)^{d}}J(q)J(-q)\int\frac{d^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{p^{2}-i\epsilon}\frac{1}{(p-q)^{2}-i\epsilon}.

(154)

We wrote the above expression in general
d
-space time dimensions in order to regulate the divergence appearing
d=4
. This allows us to evaluate the above expression and obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\epsilon}\frac{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{d}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}2^{3-d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\int\frac{d^{d}q}{(2\pi)^{d}}J(q)J(-q)(q^{2}-i\varepsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}-2}.

(155)

Focusing on the case of
d=4-\epsilon
and expanding in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit we get

W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]=\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}J(q)J(-q)\left(\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon}+\frac{2-\gamma_{E}}{16\pi^{2}}-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\log\left(\frac{q^{2}}{4\pi\mu^{2}}\right)\right).

(156)

Let us now use the
\overline{MS}
renormalization scheme. In this scheme we define the counter term in such a way that the pole at
\epsilon=0
together with the constant term in (
156
) are cancelled. As a result we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}J(q)J(-q)\log\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right).

(157)

In position space the above non-local renormalized connected functional reads as

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,\eta_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int d^{4}x\ J(x)\log\left(\frac{-\partial^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)J(x).

(158)

Let us now promote the above expression to curved background, namely

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{\text{source}}[J,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ J(x)\log\left(-\frac{\square+\ldots}{\mu^{2}}\right)J(x).

(159)

Here the ellipses stand for a term linear in Ricci scalar which is required to obtain the following homogeneous Weyl transformation property
14
14

14

For instance, in the case of free massless scalar
\Phi(x)
one can show explicitly that
\left(\square-\frac{1}{6}R\right)\Phi(x)\overset{\text{Weyl}}{\longrightarrow}e^{-3\sigma(x)}\left(\square-\frac{1}{6}R\right)\Phi(x)
.

\log\left(-\frac{\square+\ldots}{\mu^{2}}\right)J(x)\overset{\text{Weyl}}{\longrightarrow}e^{-2\sigma(x)}\log\left(-e^{-2\sigma(x)}\frac{\square+\ldots}{\mu^{2}}\right)J(x).

(160)

It is not important to find this curvature correction term as it vanishes in flat spacetime.
As a result the Weyl variation of the connected functional (
159
) is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}W_{\text{source}}[J,g_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{J}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ \sigma(x)\ J^{2}(x),

(161)

where

\displaystyle\displaystyle c_{J}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}.

(162)

As we can see the variation (
161
) is non-zero, in other words we have an extra Weyl anomaly described by the coefficient
c_{J}
called the “coupling space” anomaly.

Compensated massive scalar theory

Let us now consider the action (
147
) in the special case when the source is given by

J(x)=-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\Omega(x)^{2},

(163)

where
\Omega(x)=e^{-\tau(x)}
is the compensator field. Then the action (
147
) agrees with the compensated free theory action (
50
) in
d=4
.

This action precisely describes the compensated massive scalar theory (
50
) studied in section
4.1
. Let us consider the following IR effective action

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\text{IR}}[\tau,g_{\mu\nu}]=\frac{m^{4}}{4}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}e^{-4\tau(x)}\left(\lambda+c_{J}\ \tau(x)\right).

(164)

It is constructed in such a way that its Weyl transformation has exactly the same form as (
161
). The very first term here is invariant under Weyl transformations. We can expand this action in power of
\tau(x)
, we get then

A_{\text{IR}}[\tau,\eta_{\mu\nu}]=\frac{m^{4}}{4}\int d^{4}x\ \Bigg(\lambda+\tau(c_{J}-4\lambda)+\tau^{2}(8\lambda-4c_{J})\\
+\tau^{3}\left(8c_{J}-\frac{32\lambda}{3}\right)+O\left(\tau^{4}\right)\Bigg).

(165)

Let us define the following vertices

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)(k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n})}V_{(\tau\ldots\tau)}(k_{1},\ldots k_{n})\equiv(2\pi)^{4n}\frac{i\delta^{n}A_{\text{IR}}}{\delta\tau(k_{1})\ldots\delta\tau(k_{n})}.

(166)

Using the above effective action we obtain the following explicit expressions

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(\tau)}(k_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{im^{4}}{4}(c_{J}-4\lambda),

(167)

\displaystyle V_{(\tau\tau)}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{im^{4}}{2}(8\lambda-4c_{J}),

(168)

\displaystyle V_{(\tau\tau\tau)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3im^{4}}{2}\left(8c_{J}-\frac{32\lambda}{3}\right).

(169)

These vertices can be used to probe the value of
c_{J}
.

Let us now compute these vertices using the action (
50
) in flat background. This will allow us to extract the values of
\lambda
and the “coupling space” anomaly
c_{J}
. We will get precisely the result (
162
) which confirms the consistency of our discussion.

Let us write here the action (
50
) for convenience (which is equivalent to (
147
) combined with (
163
)), it reads

A_{\text{QFT}}[\Phi,\tau]=\int d^{4-\epsilon}x\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x)\partial_{\nu}\Phi(x)\\
-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\left(1-2\tau(x)+2\tau(x)^{2}-\frac{4}{3}\tau^{3}+O\left(\tau^{4}\right)\right)\Phi^{2}(x)\Big).

(170)

Using this action we can derive the following Feynman rules

\mathbf{J}\left(a,b;\Delta\right)
