\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Evaluating these diagrams we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(\tau\tau)}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2m^{2}\ \mathbb{J}(0,1;m^{2})+2m^{4}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \mathbb{J}(0,2;m^{2}+x(1-x)k_{1}^{2})

(172)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{im^{4}}{2\pi^{2}\epsilon}-\frac{im^{4}}{8\pi^{2}}\left((2\gamma_{E}-1)+2\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}\right)\right)+O(k_{1}^{2}).

Comparing the expressions for the above two vertices with (
167
) and (
168
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\displaystyle c_{J}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}},\qquad\ \lambda=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon}+\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\left(3-2\gamma_{E}-2\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}\right)\right).

(173)

The above
c_{J}
value agrees with the UV result in (
162
). Therefore we see that the “coupling space” trace anomaly is matched. Note that the computation above is performed using the bare action (
170
). However, it is important to emphasize that the choice of regularization scheme and counter terms will only impact the constant
\lambda
and not the result of
c_{J}
derived above.

As a consistency check let us also compute the three-point vertex. It is given by the sum of the following diagrams

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}

k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{2}

k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{1}

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
\ell

Evaluating these diagrams we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(\tau\tau\tau)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4m^{2}\ \mathbb{J}(0,1;m^{2})-4m^{4}\int_{0}^{1}dx\ \Big[\mathbb{J}\left(0,2;m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}\right)

(174)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{J}\left(0,2;m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{2}+k_{3})^{2}\right)+\mathbb{J}\left(0,2;m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{3})^{2}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle+16m^{6}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \mathbb{J}\left(0,3;m^{2}+x(1-x)(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}+y(1-y)k_{1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2im^{4}}{\pi^{2}\epsilon}+\frac{im^{4}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\gamma_{E}+\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}\right)\right)+O(k_{i}.k_{j},k_{i}^{2}).

The above result is consistent with (
169
) and (
173
).

7
A Few Open Questions

•

There has been a lot of recent progress in the field of the
S
-matrix bootstrap, see
Kruczenski:2022lot
for a review.
The general direction of extracting central charges from the
S
-matrix bootstrap was initiated in
Karateev:2019ymz
, see also
Karateev:2020axc
. The special case of 4d was studied in
Karateev:2022jdb
, see also
Marucha:2023vrn
for further numerical explorations.
15
15

15

In a similar way one can study the chiral anomaly. For the recent applications in large
N_{c}
QCD see
Albert:2023jtd
;
Ma:2023vgc
.
The idea of
Karateev:2022jdb
is to consider a set of scattering amplitudes which consists not only of physical particles, but also of background dilaton fields. The latter carry information about
a_{\text{UV}}
. This setup for instance allowed to put lower bounds on
a_{\text{UV}}
given some assumptions about the IR spectrum of particles and their interactions.
The results of our present paper would allow to extend
Karateev:2022jdb
by adding the graviton particle into the
S
-matrix setup.

•

In
Niarchos:2020nxk
;
Andriolo:2022lcb
the question of type-B trace anomaly matching in some supersymmetric theories was discussed. It would be nice to reconsider those examples in light of our determination of the universal amplitudes that probe the anomalies.

•

It would be nice to test our general predictions for the universal amplitudes in interacting theories. For a class of weakly coupled theories this will appear in
Karateev
.

•

The
a
- and
c
-anomalies are matched because they are
c
-number violations of Weyl invariance – i.e. they cannot depend on expectation values of local operators or on the state of the system. We have discussed how these anomalies are matched in Lorentz invariant vacua of the theory (which may or may not be gapped). The question of how to match these anomalies in other states of the system is far less explored. For the thermal state see
Eling:2013bj
;
Benjamin:2023qsc
.

•

The
a
-anomaly cannot depend on exactly marginal coupling constants due to the Wess-Zumino consistency condition but the
c
-anomaly may well depend on such couplings
Nakayama:2017oye
. This could arise in some large
N
non-supersymmetric fixed points with exactly marginal couplings, such as
Chaudhuri:2020xxb
(however, in this model the
c
-anomaly does not appear to depend on the exactly marginal couplings
Nakayama:2021fgy
). This would be nice to explore further. A more general class of trace anomalies associated to exactly marginal couplings are the Zamolodchikov anomalies
Osborn:1991gm
;
Friedan:2012hi
;
Gomis:2015yaa
that again need to be matched. It would be interesting to explore these further.

•

In renormalization group flows between
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetric fixed points, since
a=c
, we will find that our helicity flipping amplitude vanishes at small
t
. In the AdS dual, this vanishing of the graviton-dilaton helicity-violating scattering should be understood from the coupling of the
D3
-brane to gravity, and with higher derivative terms, it should be possible to activate this amplitude. We leave this to the future.

•

The monotonicity properties of the renormalization group remain elusive in six dimensions
Elvang:2012st
;
Heckman:2015axa
;
Cordova:2015fha
;
Stergiou:2016uqq
;
Osborn:2015rna
;
Grinstein:2014xba
. It would be interesting to throw the graviton into the mix and try to understand the space of amplitudes (four- and six-point functions). Similarly, coupling the dilaton to point-like and extended impurities in space (i.e. line defect in space-time or surface operators and higher dimensional defects in space-time) has led to new results on renormalization group flows on defects
Jensen:2015swa
;
Wang:2020xkc
;
Wang:2021mdq
;
Cuomo:2021rkm
and it would be interesting to understand what other correlators are protected by the defect monotonic function.

Appendix A
Review of Weyl Anomalies

A.1
Conventions

The metric is denoted by
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
. The symbol
g^{\mu\nu}
defines the inverse metric, which is defined through the following relation

g^{\mu\nu}g_{\nu\rho}\equiv\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}.

(175)

The object
\sqrt{-g}
is defined as

\sqrt{-g}\equiv\sqrt{-\det(g_{\mu\nu})}.

(176)

The Christoffel symbol
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\rho}
is defined as

\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{4}}\left(\partial_{\mu_{3}}g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}+\partial_{\mu_{2}}g_{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}-\partial_{\mu_{4}}g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\right).

(177)

The Riemann tensor is defined as

R^{\mu_{1}}{}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}\equiv\partial_{\mu_{3}}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}-\partial_{\mu_{4}}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}+\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\mu_{3}\mu_{5}}\Gamma^{\mu_{5}}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}-\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\mu_{4}\mu_{5}}\Gamma^{\mu_{5}}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}.

(178)

The Ricci tensor is defined as

R_{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}\equiv R^{\mu_{1}}{}_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{4}}.

(179)

The Ricci scalar is defined as

R\equiv g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}R_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}.

(180)

The squared Weyl tensor in 4d can be written as

\mathcal{W}^{2}=R^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}R_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}-2R^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}R_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\frac{1}{3}R^{2}.

(181)

The Euler density in 4d is given by

E_{4}=R^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}R_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}-4R^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}R_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+R^{2}.

(182)

Let also write explicitly the definition of
\square\tau
object in curved space-time. It reads as

\square\tau\equiv(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}-g^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}})\partial_{\mu}\tau(x).

(183)

In the
\kappa
expansion we can write the following main quantities

\displaystyle\sqrt{-\text{det}\ g}

\displaystyle=1+\kappa h+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}(h^{2}-2h^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu})+\frac{\kappa^{3}}{6}(h^{3}-6hh^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}+8h^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho\nu}h^{\nu}_{\ \mu})+O(\kappa^{4}),

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mu\nu}-2\kappa h^{\mu\nu}+4\kappa^{2}h^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho}^{\ \nu}-8\kappa^{3}h^{\mu\lambda}h_{\lambda\rho}h^{\rho\nu}+O(\kappa^{4}),

(184)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(\kappa\eta^{\lambda\sigma}-2\kappa^{2}h^{\lambda\sigma}+4\kappa^{3}h^{\lambda\rho}h_{\rho}^{\ \sigma})\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\nu\sigma}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\sigma\mu}-\partial_{\sigma}h_{\mu\nu}\big)+\ O(\kappa^{4}),

where
h=\eta^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}
.
For deriving interaction vertices involving fermions in section
4.2
we also need to the provide the expansion of vielbeins and spin connection

\displaystyle\displaystyle e_{\mu}^{\ a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}^{a}+\kappa h_{\mu}^{\ a}-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}h_{\rho}^{\ a}h_{\mu}^{\ \rho}+\frac{\kappa^{3}}{2}h_{\rho}^{\ a}h_{\sigma}^{\ \rho}h_{\mu}^{\ \sigma}+\ O(\kappa^{4}),

\displaystyle E_{a}^{\ \mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{a}^{\mu}-\kappa h_{a}^{\ \mu}+\frac{3}{2}\kappa^{2}h_{a}^{\ \rho}h_{\rho}^{\ \mu}-\frac{5}{2}\kappa^{3}h_{a}^{\ \rho}h_{\rho\sigma}h^{\sigma\mu}+\ O(\kappa^{4}),

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa(\partial^{b}h_{\mu}^{\ a}-\partial^{a}h_{\mu}^{\ b})+\kappa^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}h^{b\sigma}\partial_{\mu}h_{\sigma}^{\ a}-\frac{1}{2}h^{a\sigma}\partial_{\mu}h_{\sigma}^{\ b}+h^{b\sigma}\partial^{a}h_{\mu\sigma}-h^{a\sigma}\partial^{b}h_{\mu\sigma}

(185)

\displaystyle\ +h^{a\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}h_{\mu}^{\ b}-h^{b\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}h_{\mu}^{\ a}\Big)+\ O(\kappa^{3}).

A.2
Weyl Anomalies

Define the infinitesimal Weyl transformation of the connected functional of a conformal field theory as

\delta_{\sigma}W_{\text{CFT}}[g_{\mu\nu}]\equiv W_{\text{CFT}}\left[e^{2\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}\right]-W_{\text{CFT}}[g_{\mu\nu}].

(186)

The Weyl transformation (
186
) forms an Abelian group. Thus, Weyl transformations must commute, namely

\big[\delta_{\sigma_{1}},\,\delta_{\sigma_{2}}\big]W_{\text{CFT}}[g_{\mu\nu}]=0,

(187)

known as the Wess-Zumino consistency condition. The most general solution has three parameters and is given by

\delta_{\sigma}W_{\text{CFT}}[g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\ \sigma(x)\big(-a_{\text{CFT}}\,E_{4}(x)+c_{\text{CFT}}\,\mathcal{W}^{2}(x)+b\square R\big),

(188)

where
E_{4}(x)
is the Euler density and
\mathcal{W}^{2}(x)
is the squared Weyl tensor.
The constants
a_{\text{CFT}}
and
c_{\text{CFT}}
are called the
a
- and
c
-anomalies respectively. In a CFT, they appear as the OPE coefficients in the three-point function of the stress-tensor. For a recent review see section 2.1 in
Karateev:2022jdb
. The coefficient
b
is scheme dependent and hence is not part of the universal data in the continuum. It is possible to add the following counterterm

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}R^{2}

(189)

to the original CFT action and remove the coefficient
b
.
16
16

16

Under infinitesimal Weyl transformation,
\delta_{\sigma}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}R^{2}=-12\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}(\square\sigma)R
.

The
c
-anomaly is of “type-B.” It was first discovered in
Capper:1974ic
, and the corresponding non-local connected functional was formulated in
Deser:1976yx
. The development of the connected functional associated with the “type-A”
a
-anomaly was initiated in
Riegert:1984kt
and eventually resolved in
Deser:1993yx
.

Appendix B
Interaction between the Dilaton and the IR CFT

We address some important details about the situation when there is a nontrivial infrared conformal field theory. The dilaton and metric would couple to these infrared degrees of freedom and it is important to understand the contribution to the various vertices we considered in the main text. The coupling to the metric and dilaton is constrained by requiring Weyl and diffeomorphism invariance.
Weyl invariance can be made manifest by using the rescaled metric as before,

\widehat{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv e^{-2\tau(x)}g_{\mu\nu}(x)
and, for primary operators, we rescale them according to their scaling dimension
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mu\nu\ldots}_{\Delta}(x)\equiv e^{\Delta\tau(x)}\mathcal{O}^{\mu\nu\ldots}_{\Delta}(x)
.

Another very important requirement comes from the fact that we have assumed that, in the absence of background fields, the renormalization group flow leads to the infrared CFT. Therefore, when we set the background fields to be trivial, we should not find relevant operators in flat space.

Consider first a scalar irrelevant operator and its simplest coupling to the background fields:

A_{\text{interaction}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-\widehat{g}}\ M^{4-\Delta}\ \widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}(x),

(190)

This will contribute to
V_{\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi}
schematically as

\displaystyle\frac{M^{8-2\Delta}}{f^{4}}\int d^{4}x\int d^{4}y\varphi^{2}(x)\varphi^{2}(x)\ \langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(y)\rangle_{\text{IR CFT}}

(191)

\displaystyle\qquad\to\frac{M^{4}}{f^{4}}\left[\left(\frac{s}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}+\left(\frac{t}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}+\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}\right],

which is more suppressed than four powers of momentum for
\Delta>4
. Indeed any irrelevant operator dressed by the dilaton is suppressed by the scale
M
of the RG flow and hence the corresponding amplitudes are subleading in the deep IR and, thus, can be ignored.
17
17

17

From dimensional arguments, the coupling of irrelevant operators has an IR scale dependence of
M^{-\alpha}
, with
\alpha>0
. As a consequence, they contribute to our vertices and amplitudes at orders greater than four powers of momenta, as in the example above.

It can be quickly seen that CFT operators with spin
\ell\geq 1
once appropriately contracted with the curvature tensors will only produce irrelevant terms. We conclude that the only terms which we need to worry about read as

A_{\text{interaction}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]=\sum_{1\leq\Delta\leq 2}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-\widehat{g}}\ M^{2-\Delta}R(\widehat{g})\ \widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}(x),

(192)

where
\mathcal{O}
are scalar primary operators whose scaling dimensions are restricted to the range
1\leq\Delta\leq 2
. This expression has been originally found in
Luty:2012ww
. Upon setting the metric to be trivial and the dilaton to be a constant, the terms in (
192
) all vanish.

Now we substitute
\tau(x)
in terms of field
\varphi(x)
and metric in terms of traceless and transverse graviton field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
from (
19
) in the above expression. Up to the quadratic order in dilaton and/or graviton fields the action becomes

\displaystyle A_{\text{interaction}}[\Omega,\,g_{\mu\nu}]

(193)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq\Delta\leq 2}M^{2-\Delta}\int d^{4}x\Bigg(\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{f}\partial^{2}\varphi+\frac{3}{f^{2}}(\Delta-1)\varphi\partial^{2}\varphi+\kappa^{2}(4h^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}h_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+3\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\mu}h^{\alpha\beta}-2\partial^{\beta}h^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta})-\frac{6\sqrt{2}\kappa}{f}h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\varphi+\ \cdots\Bigg)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x).

Since the terms that involve only the dilaton contain
\partial^{2}\varphi
, they do not contribute upon imposing
\partial^{2}\varphi=0
as we do in the main body of the text. In particular, the vertices
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
,
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
are not affected by the couplings of the background fields to the gapless infrared modes.
Furthermore, for a similar reason, the vertex
V_{(hh\varphi)}
is not affected by these couplings. To see that, remember that the metric also couples to the operators of the infrared CFT via
\kappa\int d^{4}x\ h_{\mu\nu}(x)T^{\mu\nu}(x)+O(\kappa^{2})
, but since the two-point function of the energy momentum tensor and the scalar primaries vanishes, there is no contribution to the vertex
V_{(hh\varphi)}
. The conclusion from this discussion is that with the constraints on the background fields that we have imposed, there are no additional contributions from the infrared CFT to the vertices
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
,
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
, and
V_{(hh\varphi)}
.
Finally, it is important to discuss the vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
. Under the condition
\partial^{2}\varphi=0
the only contribution is proportional to

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\sum_{1\leq\Delta\leq 2}M^{4-2\Delta}\int d^{4}x\int d^{4}y\ h^{\mu\nu}(x)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\varphi(x)h^{\rho\sigma}(y)\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\varphi(y)\times\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(y)\rangle_{\text{IR CFT}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}\sum_{1\leq\Delta\leq 2}\left[(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})\left(\frac{t}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{4})+(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{2}.k_{3})\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{4})\right]

(194)

where we use the notation of section
3
.
Comparing with (
44
), we conclude that each operator of dimension
\Delta
gives the following contribution

\displaystyle g_{6}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\to g_{6}(s,t,u)+i\frac{144\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}

(195)

\displaystyle g_{8}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\to g_{8}(s,t,u)+i\frac{144\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}\left[\left(\frac{t}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}+\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}\right]

(196)

\displaystyle g_{9}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\to g_{9}(s,t,u)-i\frac{288\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}\left(\frac{t}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}\,,

(197)

where
r_{\Delta}
is a numerical constant that will not be important in what follows.
In particular, for
\Delta=2
this contributes to the
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
amplitude in the same kinematical structure as the
r_{1}
coefficient and it amounts to the shift
r_{1}\to r_{1}+r_{\Delta}
.
18
18

18

More precisely, when
\Delta=2
the integrals are logarithmically divergent and one needs to introduce a regularization scheme (indeed
r_{\Delta}\propto\frac{1}{2-\Delta}
).
In practice, one should replace
r_{\Delta}\Big(\frac{-p^{2}}{M^{2}}\Big)^{\Delta-2}\rightarrow\ln\Big(\frac{-p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\Big)
, where
\mu
is a renormalization scale.
This is related with the coupling space anomaly discussed in section
6
.

Therefore, it does not affect the extraction of the
a
and
c
anomalies from this vertex.

We also argue that the result of the 2-graviton-2-dilaton amplitude in (
135
) will not be affected by the presence of any low-dimension scalar operator at the IR fixed point. Note that in the computation of (
135
), we used the graviton-dilaton-dilaton EFT vertex
V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
in (
32
) for off-shell dilatons, two dilaton vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi)}
for off-shell dilatons in (
116
), three dilaton vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
for two on-shell dilatons and one off-shell dilaton, and the vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
for on-shell dilatons, as evident from the set of diagrams below (
126
).
Above we discussed the effect of an operator of dimension
\Delta<2
at the IR fixed point, on the on-shell vertex
V_{(hh\varphi\varphi)}
.
The off-shell vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi)}
will also be affected. Namely, equation (
116
) will change via
r_{1}\to r_{1}+r_{\Delta}(-p^{2}/M^{2})^{\Delta-2}
. The off-shell vertex
V_{(\varphi\varphi\varphi)}
can receive contribution both from
\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
, but both the contributions vanish when we set any two of the dilatons on-shell.
The vertex
V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
for off-shell dilatons will receive contributions from both
\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
and
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
.
The contribution of
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
will affect (
32
) in such a way that it will be non-vanishing only when both dilatons are off-shell. However, as observed from the set of Feynman diagrams below (
126
), the only contribution needed from the vertex
V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
is when one or both dilatons are on-shell. Therefore, we can ignore the contribution from
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
.
The two-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle
will lead to an extra contribution of the form

\displaystyle-i\frac{72\kappa}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}\left[(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})k_{3}^{2}\left(\frac{-k_{3}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}+(k_{3}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{3})k_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{-k_{2}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}\right]

(198)

to
(\varepsilon_{1})_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
given in (
32
).
Here we assumed that the graviton is on-shell because this is sufficient to compute the amplitude (
134
). Notice that the only diagram with an off-shell (internal) graviton contributing to the amplitude (
134
) involves

V_{(h\varphi\varphi)}
with two on-shell dilatons. It is easy to see that (
193
) cannot contribute in that case.

We now compute the contribution to the amplitude by summing up the diagrams below (
126
). In order to do that, we first write down the extra contribution from a low dimension scalar operator to the relevant vertices in the conventions used in section
5
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(\varphi\varphi)}(p,-p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{f^{2}}\times 36r_{\Delta}(p^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2},

(199)

\displaystyle V^{\mu\nu}_{(h\varphi\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{72\kappa}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}\left[k_{2}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}\ k_{3}^{2}\left(\frac{-k_{3}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}+k_{3}^{\mu}k_{3}^{\nu}\ k_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{-k_{2}^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}\right],

(200)

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\frac{144\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4})\left(\frac{s}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}

(201)

\displaystyle+i\frac{144\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{4})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2}

The contribution of each diagram below (
126
) reads

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{144i\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{4})(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{2})\left(\frac{s}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2},

(202)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{2}=-i\mathcal{T}_{3},

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{144i\kappa^{2}}{f^{2}}r_{\Delta}(k_{2}.\varepsilon_{3}.k_{2})(k_{4}.\varepsilon_{1}.k_{4})\left(\frac{u}{M^{2}}\right)^{\Delta-2},

(203)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{5}=-i\mathcal{T}_{6},

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(204)

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{8}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{(h\varphi h\varphi)}(k_{1},k_{2},-k_{3},-k_{4};\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3}),

(205)

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{10}=0.

(206)

Therefore, the contribution to the amplitude vanishes because

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{I=1}^{10}\mathcal{T}_{I}=0.

(207)

Appendix C
Graviton-Dilaton Scattering in the Center of Mass

In this appendix we study the graviton-dilaton scattering amplitude

h(p_{1},\lambda_{1})\varphi(p_{2})\longrightarrow h(p_{3},\lambda_{3})\varphi(p_{4})

(208)

in the center of mass frame. Here we denote the gravitons by
h
and the dilatons by
\varphi
. The four-momenta are denoted by
p_{i}
and the helitcities of the gravitons are denoted by
\lambda_{i}=\pm 2
. In this appendix we apply the formalism of
Hebbar:2020ukp
in order to study the process (
208
).
19
19

19

The problem of studying scattering amplitudes of particles with spin was addressed in the 60s by many authors
Jacob:1959at
;
Trueman:1964zzb
;
Hara:1970gc
;
Hara:1971kj
, see the older review
Martin:102663
for a more comprehensive list. See also
Bellazzini:2016xrt
;
deRham:2017zjm
for more recent discussions.

Both the graviton and the dilaton are massless.
The centre of mass frame is defined as

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{p},0,0,+\boldsymbol{p}),

(209)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\text{com}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{p},0,0,-\boldsymbol{p}),

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{p},+\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta,0,+\boldsymbol{p}\cos\theta),

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\text{com}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{p},-\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}\cos\theta).

Here
\theta
is the scattering angle on the
x-z
plane.
The sine and cosine of the scattering angle are related to the Mandelstam variables as

\boldsymbol{p}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},\qquad\sin\theta=\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s},\qquad\cos\theta=\frac{t-u}{s}.

(210)

These can be inverted and lead to

t=-\frac{s}{2}(1-\cos\theta),\qquad u=-\frac{s}{2}(1+\cos\theta).

(211)

The Mandelstam variables are related as

s+t+u=0.

(212)

C.1
Scattering and Partial Amplitudes

The scattering process (
208
) is described by the scattering amplitude

(2\pi)^{2}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})\times\mathcal{S}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\equiv{}_{\text{out}}\langle h(p_{3},\lambda_{3})\varphi(p_{4})|h(p_{1},\lambda_{1})\varphi(p_{2})\rangle_{\text{in}},

(213)

where the helicity placed downstairs is associated with the incoming graviton and the helicity placed upstairs is associated with the outgoing graviton. The amplitude (
213
) can be split into the trivial and the interacting parts as

\mathcal{S}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\left[\frac{2p_{1}^{0}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{p}_{1}-\vec{p}_{3})2p_{2}^{0}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{p}_{2}-\vec{p}_{4})}{(2\pi)^{2}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})}\right]\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}\\
+i\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}).

(214)

The centre of mass amplitudes are defined as

\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s,t,u)\equiv\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(p^{\text{com}}_{1},p^{\text{com}}_{2},p^{\text{com}}_{3},p^{\text{com}}_{4}).

(215)

We assume that parity is a symmetry of our theory, then the following relations hold

\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{-2}^{-2}(s,t,u),\qquad\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{-2}^{+2}(s,t,u).

(216)

It follows from equation (2.64) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
.
As a result there are only two independent amplitudes.
Crossing for massless particles, see equation (2.82) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
, simply implies that both of these amplitude are
s-u
symmetric, namely

\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(u,t,s),\qquad\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(u,t,s).

(217)

This is no longer the case if one of the particles is massive. Then, there is mixing between the two amplitudes.

Let us now consider the two-particle state projected to the definite angular momentum

\displaystyle|p,\ell,\lambda_{1}\rangle_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(|h(p_{1},\lambda_{1})\varphi(p_{2})\rangle_{\text{in}}\right)_{\text{projected to $\ell$}},

(218)

\displaystyle{}_{\text{out}}\langle p^{\prime},\ell,\lambda_{3}|

\displaystyle\equiv\left({}_{\text{out}}\langle h(p_{3},\lambda_{3})\varphi(p_{4})|\right)_{\text{projected to $\ell$}}.

(219)

Here
p=p_{1}+p_{2}
and
p^{\prime}=p_{3}+p_{4}
. When both states are in or out projected states they are normalized as

\langle p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}|p,\ell,\lambda\rangle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p-p^{\prime})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}.

(220)

The partial amplitudes of the process (
208
) is defined as

[\mathcal{S}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s)\equiv{}\text{out}\langle p^{\prime},\ell,\lambda_{3}|p,\ell,\lambda_{1}\rangle_{\text{in}}.

(221)

The above definition leads to the following expression

[\mathcal{S}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s)=\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}+i[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s),

(222)

where
\ell=2,3,4,\ldots
is the angular momentum and

[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s)=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\,d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}(\theta)\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}\left(s,\,t=-\frac{s}{2}(1-\cos\theta),\,u=-\frac{s}{2}(1+\cos\theta)\right).

(223)

For details see equations (2.106) and (2.108) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
.
Here
d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}(\theta)
is the Wigner small
d
-matrix. Due to parity we also have the following relation

[\mathcal{S}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s)=[\mathcal{S}_{\ell}]{}_{-\lambda_{1}}^{-\lambda_{3}}(s).

(224)

Using the orthogonality of the Wigner’s small
d
-matrix we can invert the relation (
223
). The result reads as

\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s,t,u)=4\pi\sum_{\ell\geq 2}(2\ell+1)[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3}}(s)d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}(\theta).

(225)

Let us evaluate this relation in the forward limit
\theta=0
. Using the following properties of the Wigner’s small
d
-matrix

\ell\geq 2:\qquad d^{(\ell)}_{+2+2}(0)=1,\qquad d^{(\ell)}_{+2-2}(0)=0,

(226)

we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{+2}(s,0,-s)

\displaystyle=4\pi\sum_{\ell\geq 2}(2\ell+1)[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s),

(227)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)

\displaystyle=0.

In writing these relations we only assumed that the forward limit for the amplitudes exist. This type of arguments was also used in appendix A.4 in
Haring:2022sdp
for providing an alternative derivation of unitarity constraints in the forward limit. This is consistent with our main result, which shows that the helicity flipping amplitude vanishes in the forward limit (
2
).

An alternative derivation of the second equation in (
227
) is done by applying the logic of section 2.7 in
Hebbar:2020ukp
for the forward
t=0
limit. In the forward limit there is an enhanced
SO(2)
symmetry around the
z
-axis. Invariance under this symmetry forces the amplitude to vanish in the forward limit unless
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{3}
. For details see equation (2.140) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
and the discussion around it.

To conclude let us derive two useful relations for later. Consider expression (
225
) with
\lambda_{1}=+2
and
\lambda_{3}=-2
. Then apply one and two derivates in
t
and evaluate the result in the forward limit. Using the explicit expression of the Wigner
d
-matrix we can write

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)

\displaystyle=0,

(228)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{3s^{2}}\sum_{\ell\geq 2}(2\ell+1)(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s).

C.2
Graviton Polarizations

The graviton polarization can simply be written as a product of the photon polarization

\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}(p)=\varepsilon_{\lambda/2}^{\mu}(k_{i})\varepsilon_{\lambda/2}^{\nu}(k_{i}),

(229)

where the explicit expressions for
\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{\mu}(p)
can be found for example in equation (B.34) in
Haring:2022sdp
. For incoming photons we have

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda=\pm 1}(p)=\frac{e^{i\lambda\phi}}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\cos\theta\cos\phi-i\lambda\sin\phi\\
\cos\theta\sin\phi+i\lambda\cos\phi\\
-\sin\theta\end{pmatrix},

(230)

where
\theta
and
\phi
are the spherical angles of the photon incoming 3-momentum
\vec{p}
. The outgoing polarizations are simply obtained by complex conjugation, namely

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}\left(p\big|_{\text{outgoing}}\right)=\left(\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}\left(p\big|_{\text{incoming}}\right)\right)^{*}.

(231)

Suppose the most generic situation of scattering of four photons.
By convention we choose
\phi_{i}=0
for all four particles. Instead
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=0
for the incoming particles and
\theta_{3}=\theta
and
\theta_{4}=\pi+\theta
. Taking complex conjugate for the outgoing particles we conclude that in the center of mass the photon polarizations reads as

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda=\pm 1}(p_{1}^{\text{com}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
i\lambda\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda=\pm 1}(p_{2}^{\text{com}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-1\\
i\lambda\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(232)

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda=\pm 1}(p_{3}^{\text{com}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\cos\theta\\
-i\lambda\\
-\sin\theta\end{pmatrix},\qquad\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda=\pm 1}(p_{4}^{\text{com}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\cos\theta\\
-i\lambda\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix},

(233)

C.3
Unitarity and Positivity Constraints

It is convenient to define the following parity even and parity odd eigenstates

\displaystyle|\text{parity }+\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(|p,\ell,\lambda=+2\rangle+(-1)^{\ell}|p,\ell,\lambda=-2\rangle\right),

(234)

\displaystyle|\text{parity }-\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(|p,\ell,\lambda=+2\rangle-(-1)^{\ell}|p,\ell,\lambda=-2\rangle\right).

(235)

We also define the following short-hand notation

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell}(s)\equiv[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)+(-1)^{\ell}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s),

(236)

\displaystyle\chi_{\ell}(s)\equiv[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)-(-1)^{\ell}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s).

Let us focus on the parity plus eigenstates.
We can then form the following matrix

|\psi\rangle_{\text{in}}

|\psi\rangle_{\text{out}}

{}_{\text{in}}\langle\psi|

1

1-i\Phi_{\ell}^{*}{}(s)

{}_{\text{out}}\langle\psi|

1+i\Phi_{\ell}(s)

1

Since our theory is unitarity the above matrix is positive semidefinite. Plugging inside the decomposition (
222
) we get

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\begin{pmatrix}1&1-i\Phi_{\ell}^{*}{}(s)\\
1+i\Phi_{\ell}(s)&1\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

(237)

An analogous condition holds for the
\Phi_{-}(s)
partial amplitude.
These in turn can be rewritten as the following conditions

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\left|1+i\Phi_{\ell}(s)\right|^{2}\leq 1.

(238)

From this we can read of the positivity condition

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\text{Im}\Phi_{\ell}(s)\geq 0.

(239)

Or using (
236
) this condition can be equivalently rewritten

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)+(-1)^{\ell}\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s)\geq 0.

(240)

An analogous condition can be derived using parity odd eigenstates. We have

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)-(-1)^{\ell}\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s)\geq 0.

(241)

From the above two conditions we can conclude that

\forall\ell=2,3,4,\ldots\qquad s\geq 0:\qquad\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)\geq 0,

(242)

whereas
\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s)
does not have a definite sign and it is bounded by

\left|\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s)\right|\leq\text{Im}[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{+2}(s)
.

C.4
Dispersion Relation

Let us consider the centre of mass scattering amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
in the complex
s
plane for some fixed physical value of
t
. Assuming that this amplitude has a polynomial expansion at low energy we can write the following expression

\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{low energy}}=g_{0}+g_{1}t+g_{2}(s^{2}+u^{2})+g_{2}^{\prime}t^{2}+g_{3}(s^{3}+u^{3})+g_{3}^{\prime}t(s^{2}+u^{2})+O(s^{4}).

(243)

Due to equation (
227
) we immediately conclude that

g_{0}=0,\qquad g_{2}=0,\qquad g_{3}=0.

(244)

Let us consider a situation when the imaginary part of the amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
is generated by the presence of some massive degrees of freedom. We denote the mass of the lowest state by
m
. We also assume that neither the dilaton nor the graviton propagate in the loops. We conclude that
\text{Im}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
can be non zero for
s\geq m^{2}
. More precisely,
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
will have poles associated with single particle states and branch cuts associated with the multi-particle continuum.
The analytic structure of the amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
is presented in figure
1
.
The left cut appears at
u=4m^{2}
due to the
s-u
crossing symmetry.

s
\bullet
4m^{2}
\bullet
-t-4m^{2}

Figure 1:
The analytic structure of the amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
on the
s
complex plane for a fixed physical value of
t
. We draw the cuts associated with multi-particle states of a QFT with mass gap
m
. In general, there are also poles associated with asymptotic single particle states but we do not draw those to avoid cluttering the figure.
If the IR theory is gapless then the cuts start from
s=0
and
s=-t
.

An efficient way to derive dispersion relations for massless particles is by using the technology of
Bellazzini:2020cot
, see appendix F in
Acanfora:2023axz
for its compact review. Similar formalisms were also proposed in
Caron-Huot:2020cmc
;
Tolley:2020gtv
. (In the case of massive particles the dispersion relations were discussed in section 4 in
Chen:2022nym
.) Consider a scattering amplitude
\Phi(s,t,u)
which is
s-u
invariant and obeys
\lim_{|s|\to\infty}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\Phi(s,t,u)=0
for some fixed value of
t
.
20
20

20

This would follow from the Martin-Froissart bound
Martin:1965jj
;
Jin:1964zza
for a QFT with a mass gap.
It is also expected in gravitational scattering as recently discussed in
Haring:2022cyf
.
Neither of these results applies directly to our case because we have massless particles but the gravitons and dilatons are just probes and therefore the non-perturbative gravitational results of
Haring:2022cyf
do not apply. Tree-level gravitational interactions should obey the classical Regge growth
Chowdhury:2019kaq
which is weaker, namely

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}\frac{1}{s^{3}}\Phi(s,t,u)=0
.
However, we expect the dilaton scattering amplitudes to be softer at high energies due to the coupling to the trace of the stress tensor that vanishes in the UV limit.

One can then write the following relation

\underset{s=0,-t}{\text{Res}}\left(\frac{\Phi(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{low energy}}}{s^{2}(s+t)}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{1}{s}+\frac{1}{s+t}\right)\frac{\text{Im}\Phi(s,t,u)}{s(s+t)}.

(245)

Let us now set
\Phi(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
. Then using (
243
) we can evaluate the left-hand side in the above equation, we get

\underset{s=0,-t}{\text{Res}}\left(\frac{\Phi(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{low energy}}}{s^{2}(s+t)}\right)=2g_{2}+(2g_{3}^{\prime}-3g_{3})t+\ldots

(246)

We immediately see that
g_{2}^{\prime}
disappears from this relation.
Plugging this result into (
245
) and taking the forward limit we conclude that

g_{2}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}ds\frac{\text{Im}\Phi(s,0,-s)}{s^{3}}.

(247)

The
t=0
limit is taken in order to remove all the other terms in (
246
) hidden by ellipses.
The left-hand side of this relation is zero due to (
244
). The right-hand side is also zero due to equation (
227
).
A non-trivial dispersion relation at this level can be derived for the
g_{3}^{\prime}
coefficient.
Taking one derivative in
t
of both sides in (
245
) and using (
246
) we get

2g_{3}^{\prime}-3g_{3}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}ds\left(-\frac{3\text{Im}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{2s^{4}}+\frac{\text{Im}\partial_{t}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{s^{3}}\right).

(248)

Taking into account equations (
244
) and (
227
) we conclude that

g_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}ds\frac{\text{Im}\partial_{t}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{s^{3}}.

(249)

Let us now set
\Phi(s,t,u)=(s^{2}+u^{2})\partial_{t}^{2}\,\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
. We will assume that
\partial_{t}^{2}\,\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
decays at
s=\infty
and fixed
t
so that we can use the relation (
245
).
21
21

21

For the massive scalar and massive fermionic QFT examples, as described in section
4
, the amplitude
\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,t,u)
vanishes in the limit
|s|\rightarrow\infty
at fixed
t
.
We expect this in general because the dilaton couples to the trace of the stress tensor which vanishes in the UV limit.

Due to (
243
) we have

\underset{s=0,-t}{\text{Res}}\left(\frac{\Phi(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{low energy}}}{s^{2}(s+t)}\right)=4\left(g_{2}+g_{2}^{\prime}+(g_{3}^{\prime}-g_{3})t\right)+\ldots

(250)

Plugging it into (
245
), taking into account (
244
) and evaluating the result in the forward limit we finally conclude that

g_{2}^{\prime}=\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{\pi}\frac{\text{Im}\,\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{T}{}_{+2}^{-2}(s,0,-s)}{s}.

(251)

Plugging here (
228
) we can also rewrite this expression as

g_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{\ell\geq 2}(2\ell+1)(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)\int_{m^{2}}^{\infty}ds\frac{\text{Im}\,[\mathcal{T}_{\ell}]{}_{+2}^{-2}(s)}{s^{3}}.

(252)

Due to the discussion in the end of appendix
C.3
the integrand in this dispersion relation is not sign definite.
